## Document use

This document is the consolidated operational handoff for continuing the Project Polaris cyberrange build. It records what has been completed, what is actually running, the approved design intent, the corrected firewall policy, the finalized AI system architecture, the remaining recovery gaps, and the sequence for continuing safely. Appendix A is a copy-ready prompt for a new chat.

**Precedence:** Later validated operator evidence in this handoff supersedes conflicting statements in RUN-002 v1.31 and RUN-003 v1.0/v1.1, particularly the earlier default-Internet-deny policy, the provisional 24 GiB runtime ceiling, the previously undecided AI-node placement, and the now-corrected PROTECTED_NETWORKS alias content (see §7.3, §11.1 step 2).

### 1.1 Immediate decision posture

- Do not redesign the network merely to make a test pass; troubleshoot against the approved architecture.
- Do not revert to the obsolete assumption that active-purpose cyber VLANs must be denied Internet during normal operation.
- Cyber guests use their OPNsense .1 address as gateway and DNS. The gateway is not required to answer ping or HTTPS because the control plane is intentionally blocked.
- Internet must fail closed if OPNsense becomes unavailable; no guest may have vmbr0, a second NIC, an alternate route, or a VPN overlay that bypasses OPNsense.
- The cyberrange AI system (attack-ai and defense-ai) stays on the cyberrange Proxmox node and never crosses to the homelab AI node.
- The PROTECTED_NETWORKS alias under-coverage of 172.16.0.0/12 and most of 100.64.0.0/10 has been corrected and re-verified against the live runtime table (22 Sep 2026) — no longer an open finding.
- The next gate is recovery, host-hardening validation, and documentation — not another workload build.

## 2 Authority, terminology, and operating rules

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Active-purpose zone | CYBER_ATTACK, CYBER_WINDOWS, CYBER_LINUX, CYBER_APPS, CYBER_SIEM, CYBER_ANALYSIS, CYBER_HYBRID (VLAN 70 — Windows/Linux combination zone, infrastructure built 4 Oct 2026, no guests yet — see §11.3.1c), or CYBER_HYBRID_INTERNAL (VLAN 71 — internal-only pivot zone supporting the VM370/371 capstone, infrastructure built 4 Oct 2026, no guests yet — see §11.3.1e. **Variant of the standard policy: no CYBER_ATTACK pass rule by design — only a narrow AD-ports-only pass to CYBER_WINDOWS.**) |
| Protected networks | Trusted, private, CGNAT/Tailscale, and link-local IPv4 space that cyber guests must not reach except through a later narrow scenario rule |
| Control plane | OPNsense services on the zone gateway itself, other than the approved DNS exception |
| Bounded gate | One approved change set with purpose, precaution, exact procedure, expected result, failure action, validation, and stop conditions |
| Final workload | An intended long-lived cyberrange role, not a disposable segmentation probe |
| Host management plane | The cyberrange Proxmox host's own vmbr0/192.168.10.119 interface and its Proxmox GUI/SSH/API surface. OPNsense's segmentation does not cover this path — it only firewalls guest traffic on vmbr1. Hardening this plane is a distinct control from VLAN isolation. |

### 2.1 Operating method

1. Use GUI-first procedures for Proxmox, OPNsense, pfSense, and Windows. Use CLI only where the GUI cannot provide the necessary validation.
2. Work one gate at a time and stop for explicit approval before changing the next subsystem.
3. Provide exact commands. If an address, interface, or object is unknown, use a read-only discovery step or stop and ask.
4. Back up before bridge, firewall, identity, storage, GPU-allocation, or power-state changes.
5. Keep Kali01 and Admin01 management paths available throughout changes.
6. Minimize power cycling. Keep a VM running through its active setup and validation sequence unless a technical reason requires shutdown.
7. Treat unexpected protected-network reachability as a containment stop condition.
8. The cyberrange Proxmox host has 31.23 GiB usable RAM. Inventory is not capped; runtime is managed live by powering guests on/off as needed rather than against a fixed provisional ceiling.
9. IPv6 is disabled and unused.
10. IP Address Plan and DNS Registry changes require explicit approval.
11. GPU allocation (device passthrough assignments, LXC vs. VM boundary for GPU-sharing guests) is treated with the same change-authority discipline as IP/VLAN/firewall changes.
12. A validation gate that surfaces a real misconfiguration (e.g. an alias content typo) does not authorize fixing it in the same breath — the correction itself is a separate, explicitly approved bounded step, even when small.

## 3 Physical and logical architecture

### 3.1 Trusted home and homelab chain

Approved Northstar direction: ISP → gateway01 home pfSense → home network/Deco → firewall01 homelab pfSense → managed switch → Proxmox → OPNsense cyber firewall → isolated cyberrange VLANs. The household edge cutover remains on hold and must not be combined with cyberrange workload deployment.

| System | Address | Function / notes |
|---|---|---|
| firewall01 | 192.168.10.1 | Trusted homelab gateway upstream of the cyberrange |
| homelab Proxmox | 192.168.10.2 | Primary Proxmox management endpoint and protected validation target |
| switch01 | 192.168.10.3 | TP-Link managed switch |
| nas01 | 192.168.10.4 | Backup and ISO repository |
| docker01 | 192.168.10.20 | NPM, Vaultwarden, Homepage, Uptime Kuma, Forgejo, Portainer |
| admin01 | 192.168.10.30 | Out-of-domain admin workstation; emergency OPNsense WAN HTTPS source |
| dc01 | 192.168.10.40 | Active Directory and AD DNS |
| admin-ws | 192.168.10.41 | Primary Windows/AD administrative workstation |
| ws01 / ws02 | 192.168.10.42 / .43 | Windows user workstations |
| ca01 / ROOTCA01 | 192.168.10.44 / .45 | Issuing CA / offline standalone root CA |
| homelab AI node | Separate host (see §3.3) | Hosts homelab AI infrastructure; 16 GB GPU; intentionally not used by the cyberrange |
| cyberrange Proxmox | 192.168.10.119 | Host for VM300, VM301, VM310, VM360, planned VM311/VM351, and future cyber workloads. Sits on the trusted subnet via its own management NIC — see §2's Host management plane definition. |
| OPNsense WAN | 192.168.10.120/24 | VM300 net0 on vmbr0; upstream gateway 192.168.10.1 |

### 3.2 Cyberrange trust boundaries

- vmbr0 is trusted host management and the OPNsense WAN path. Cyber guests must never attach to it.
- vmbr1 is the internal cyber fabric, has no Proxmox host IP, and is the only bridge for cyber guests.
- VM300 is the only cyberrange guest with both vmbr0 and vmbr1 connectivity.
- VM301 Kali01 is management infrastructure on native VLAN 1, not an attack-VLAN workload.
- VM310 and future workload VMs use exactly one VirtIO NIC on vmbr1 with the VLAN tag aligned to the VMID family.
- No cyber guest receives a trusted static route, direct NAS mount, Tailscale bypass, or second NIC.
- The cyberrange Proxmox host itself (not a guest) has a management interface on the trusted 192.168.10.0/24 subnet. OPNsense's guest-traffic segmentation does not protect this path; it is covered separately under host management-plane hardening (§2, §3.3, Gate 8G1).

### 3.3 AI system architecture and GPU allocation

Finalized 22 September 2026. The homelab already runs a separate AI node (its own Proxmox host, 16 GB GPU) supporting general homelab AI infrastructure. The cyberrange AI system is a distinct, purpose-built pair of guests that intentionally does not touch that node.

**3.3.1 Placement decision**

- The cyberrange AI system (attacking and defending model support) runs entirely on the cyberrange Proxmox node. It never crosses to, mounts, or otherwise communicates with the homelab AI node or its 16 GB GPU.
- Rationale: the cyberrange intentionally hosts purposefully vulnerable and purposefully malicious content for practice. Bridging that AI workload to homelab-adjacent infrastructure would create exactly the kind of leakage path the whole cyberrange isolation design exists to prevent. Isolation was prioritized over the convenience of a larger, shared GPU pool.
- A theoretical cross-host pooled-GPU configuration (e.g. llama.cpp RPC backend or multi-node tensor-parallel serving spanning both hosts' GPUs) is technically possible but requires a live inference-traffic network path between the two hosts. Treated as an explicit non-goal, not a future gate, unless the isolation goal itself changes.

**3.3.2 Guest placement and VMIDs**

| VMID | Role | Zone | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 311 | attack-ai | VLAN 10 (CYBER_ATTACK) | Planned; LXC container; within the existing 310–319 VMID family alongside VM310. No new VLAN or VMID block required. |
| 351 | defense-ai | VLAN 50 (CYBER_SIEM) | Planned; LXC container; within the existing 350–359 VMID family, built alongside the future SIEM host VM350. |

**3.3.3 GPU allocation**

The cyberrange node will carry two GPUs once the pending hardware update lands:

**Hardware upgrade installed and confirmed, 26 Sep 2026.** The planned upgrade path (Ryzen 7 5800X3D + X570 Taichi AM4 + RX 7700 XT 12GB) landed exactly as speculated in the 22 Sep note below, with one correction: the second card is a **GTX 970 (NVIDIA, 4 GB)**, not the previously-referenced RX 580 8GB. Confirmed host specs: **Ryzen 7 5800X3D** (8c/16t) on an **ASRock X570 Taichi**, **64 GB RAM** (up from 31.23 GiB), dual GPU.

- **RX 7700 XT 12 GB** (PCI `0e:00.0`, first slot) — dedicated to the AI system, bound to the host's `amdgpu` driver. VM311 (attack-ai) and **VM351 (defense-ai, built 11 Oct 2026 — §11.3.1k)** both use this card via cgroup device passthrough (not exclusive PCI passthrough), sharing it concurrently — combined budget ≈ 12 GB, e.g. two 7–8B models at Q4–Q5 fit together, two 14B models do not; each box's Ollama swaps one model into VRAM at a time. A host-side undervolt/clock-cap (`gpu-undervolt.service`, OverDrive via `amdgpu.ppfeaturemask=0xffffffff`) suppresses the card's coil whine and persists reboots — see §11.3.1k. **ROCm-supported hardware confirmed** (gfx1101, officially listed in AMD's ROCm 7 compatibility matrix — no `HSA_OVERRIDE_GFX_VERSION` workaround needed), superseding the earlier RX 580/gfx803/Vulkan-only note, which no longer applies to the hardware actually installed.
- **GTX 970 4 GB** (PCI `0f:00.0`, second slot) — **role resolved 7 Oct 2026 (see §3.3.5).** The host console is now driven by the **RX 7700 XT** (slot 1), because the ASRock X570 Taichi BIOS offers no primary-GPU/slot selection and always POSTs on slot 1, and the 5800X3D has no iGPU — so forcing video to the second slot isn't achievable on this board. Running the AI workload on the same card that drives the console was validated as safe (the display framebuffer is a small, pre-reserved allocation a compute job cannot evict; worst case is a model not fully fitting, not a display crash). The GTX 970 is therefore **freed** and reassigned back to its originally-planned role: **non-AI GPU workload via exclusive vfio PCI passthrough (GPU password cracking, e.g. hashcat)** — now a tracked open build item in §10, to revisit later.
- Full execution detail, including the ROCm/Debian 13 incompatibility discovery and the resulting attack-ai OS rebuild, is in §3.3.5.

**3.3.4 Accepted risk: LXC shared-kernel boundary**

VM311 and VM351 are LXC containers, sharing the Proxmox host kernel rather than getting hardware-virtualized isolation. Deliberate, accepted trade for GPU-sharing concurrency:

- A container escape from either AI guest terminates at the cyberrange Proxmox host — the same destination a full-VM hypervisor escape from any other guest (e.g. VM310) would reach. LXC lowers the probability of that escape succeeding; it does not create a new destination or a larger blast-radius class.
- The cyberrange Proxmox host itself has a management interface on the trusted 192.168.10.0/24 subnet (192.168.10.119). This — not the VLAN/OPNsense segmentation — is the actual control point for both of the operator's stated risk concerns: (1) a cyberrange vulnerability creating an externally reachable path into the homelab, and (2) an accidental mistake bricking homelab or AI-node infrastructure rather than only the disposable cyberrange. OPNsense has no visibility into or control over this host-level path.
- Not a new exposure created by the AI-node decision — it already existed for every guest on the cyberrange host. Informally addressed during the AI build; formally validated under Gate 8G1 §11.1 step 6. Findings below.

**3.3.5 Hardware upgrade execution, GPU passthrough, and confirmed GPU-accelerated inference (26 Sep 2026)**

- **Kernel housekeeping first:** the hardware/OS update left the host on `7.0.14-19-pve` with no kernel pinned (the operator had unpinned `7.0.14-12-pve`, the workaround from the §3.4.4 incident, after updating). Re-pinned to the confirmed-healthy running kernel: `proxmox-boot-tool kernel pin 7.0.14-19-pve`. `7.0.14-14-pve` (the originally broken kernel from §3.4.4) is still installed and listed under "automatically selected," but is neutralized by the pin; `proxmox-boot-tool kernel remove` doesn't apply to it since it's not in the manual list — actual removal would require purging its package directly (not done; not a live risk with the pin in place).
- **GRUB `video=efifb:off` line found inert.** The operator had added `video=efifb:off video=vesafb:off initcall_blacklist=sysfb_init` to `/etc/default/grub` as a bare line rather than inside `GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX_DEFAULT`/`GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX`. Confirmed via `/proc/cmdline` that none of these params were actually applied. Not fixed — turned out to be unnecessary: `lspci -k` confirmed the RX 7700 XT (`0e:00.0`) is bound to `amdgpu` and the GTX 970 (`0f:00.0`) to `nouveau` with no conflict, and the operator's original symptom (video only from the first GPU) was resolved by physical HDMI cable placement onto the GTX 970's port, not a kernel parameter. Line left in place (inert, harmless); cleanup optional.
- **VM311 attack-ai CPU restored to 8 cores** (up from the 4-core compromise at original build time, §11.2.2 step 5), now that the host has real headroom (5800X3D 8c/16t, 64 GB RAM).
- **GPU device passthrough into CT311 (cgroup/LXC model, not exclusive PCI passthrough):** host-side device identification via `/dev/dri/by-path/` confirmed `renderD129` (`card2`) = RX 7700 XT (`0e:00.0`), `renderD128` (`card1`) = GTX 970 (`0f:00.0`). `/dev/kfd` (ROCm compute interface) confirmed present, group `render` (host GID `993`). Attached with:
  ```
  pct set 311 -dev0 /dev/dri/renderD129,gid=993 -dev1 /dev/kfd,gid=993
  ```
  Only the 7700 XT's render node and `/dev/kfd` were passed in — not the GTX 970's nodes, and not the 7700 XT's full `card2` (DRM/KMS) node, which isn't needed for headless compute.
  **`ollama` service group membership** required a matching fix inside the container (GID 993 appeared under a different local group name depending on guest OS — `kvm` on the original Debian 13 build) — added via `usermod -aG <local-group-name> ollama` plus a service restart.
- **ROCm on Debian 13 — found broken, not attempted further.** AMD's official ROCm 7.2.4 support matrix lists only Ubuntu 24.04.4/22.04.5 and RHEL 10.1/9.7 — Debian isn't on it. A live upstream bug (`ROCm/ROCm#4323`) confirms `rocm-llvm`'s installer fails on Debian 13 outright: it depends on legacy `libstdc++`/`libgcc` `-dev` packages Debian 13's repos no longer carry, with no posted workaround. **Decision: rebuild CT311 as Ubuntu 24.04 Server** (officially validated for this GPU) rather than fight Debian's broken packaging — operator's own contingency plan from the original model-sourcing discussion, exercised here.
- **CT311 rebuilt:** same specs otherwise (8 cores, 8 GiB RAM/8 GiB swap, 200 GB disk on `Cyber_VM`, vmbr1 tag 10). New IP `10.10.10.102` (VLAN 10, DHCP), network verified. GPU devices and Ollama reinstalled per the same steps above.
- **ROCm install on Ubuntu 24.04** via AMD's official repo (`repo.radeon.com/rocm/apt/7.2.4`, `noble`), installing userspace packages only (`rocm-hip-runtime rocminfo rocm-smi-lib`) — deliberately not the full `rocm` meta-package, which commonly pulls in `amdgpu-dkms` (a kernel-module build inappropriate for an LXC sharing the host kernel).
  - **Package conflict found and fixed:** Ubuntu 24.04's own default repos ship an old, unrelated `rocminfo`/`rocm-smi` (`5.7.1-3build1`) that dpkg's version comparison treats as newer than AMD's actual current package (oddly versioned `1.0.0.70204-93~24.04`), blocking install of `rocm-hip-runtime`. Fixed by explicitly forcing the exact AMD-repo version: `apt install -y rocminfo=1.0.0.70204-93~24.04 rocm-hip-runtime rocm-smi-lib`.
  - **`rocm-smi` remains broken** even on the correct package — crashes with a clock-frequency parsing assertion (`get_multiplier_from_str`), a known class of bug on some RDNA3 cards. **Not a blocker**: `rocminfo` correctly enumerates the GPU, and Ollama talks to the GPU via the HIP runtime directly, not via `rocm-smi`. Left as a known cosmetic/monitoring gap, not pursued further.
- **GPU-accelerated inference confirmed working, 26 Sep 2026.** Ollama's own log on restart shows clean detection: `ROCm0 name=ROCm0 description="AMD Radeon RX 7700 XT" libdirs=ollama,rocm_v7_2 driver=0.0 pci_id=0000:0e:00.0 type=discrete total="12.0 GiB" available="11.8 GiB"`. Test run (`ollama run dolphin3:8b`) confirmed via `ollama ps`: **`100% GPU`**, no CPU fallback.
- **All eight candidate models re-pulled** on the rebuilt Ubuntu 24.04 CT311 and confirmed present (same roster and sourcing as §11.2.2 step 5's original staging). Attack-ai is now fully GPU-accelerated, not just staged.
- **Open item carried to §10:** the GTX 970's role (host console vs. the originally-planned non-AI-GPU-workload passthrough) is undecided — see §3.3.3.

**3.3.6 Host display path resolved — RX 7700 XT is the host console; GTX 970 freed (7 Oct 2026)**

Revisited the host-display question (video had regressed to "no output on the GTX 970 at all," most likely because a BIOS/firmware update reset primary-graphics to default). Root-caused and resolved:

- **The ASRock X570 Taichi has no primary-GPU / slot-selection BIOS option** (only 4G-decoding / Re-Size BAR, which require disabling CSM), and the 5800X3D has **no iGPU**, so the board always POSTs on the slot-1 card (RX 7700 XT). Confirmed the GTX 970 (slot 2) showed nothing at POST, through early boot, or at the OS console.
- **OS-console framebuffer diagnosis:** `cat /sys/class/graphics/fb*/name` showed `fb0 = amdgpudrmfb` (7700 XT, headless by intent) and `fb1 = nouveaudrmfb` (GTX 970, monitor attached). `fbcon` binds `fb0` by default, so the console followed the 7700 XT. An attempt to redirect it — `video=efifb:off initcall_blacklist=sysfb_init fbcon=map:1` applied correctly inside `GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX_DEFAULT` this time — did **not** move the console (disabling efifb most likely reordered the DRM framebuffers, so `map:1` no longer pointed at nouveau). Judged not worth further `fbcon=map` trial-and-error for a headless host reached by web GUI/SSH.
- **Decision: let the RX 7700 XT be the host console** (monitor on slot 1). Under the LXC cgroup-sharing model, `amdgpu` is already loaded on the host and owns the card, so a console on it does not conflict with AI compute. **Validated safe:** the display framebuffer is a small (tens-of-MB), pre-reserved allocation that compute jobs cannot evict — a maxed inference job cannot "crash" video; the only VRAM-pressure outcome is a model not fully fitting (spilling to CPU), and display power/scheduling is serviced independently of compute. Lone caveat: a rare `amdgpu` GPU hang/reset under load would momentarily reset the console too (transient, host stays reachable headless) — acceptable for a server console.
- **GRUB reverted to clean** (`GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX_DEFAULT="quiet"`, `update-grub`), dropping the `video=efifb:off`/`fbcon` params so nothing is left to interfere with a future vfio passthrough. The old inert bare-line params from §3.3.5 are superseded by this clean state.
- **GTX 970 is now free**, reassigned to its original intended role (non-AI GPU workload via exclusive vfio passthrough — hashcat), tracked as an open build item in §10.

### 3.4 Gate 8G1 step 6 — host management-plane audit findings (22 Sep 2026)

The cyberrange Proxmox host's own management-plane exposure was audited per §11.1 step 6: what can reach its GUI/SSH/API, and what standing credentials, mounts, or trust relationships it holds toward other trusted infrastructure. Five findings, each with the operator's explicit decision recorded.

**3.4.1 Proxmox cluster membership (accepted risk, partially mitigated)**

- The cyberrange Proxmox host is a member of a real Proxmox cluster, Datacenter **"polaris"**, alongside at least the `homelab` and `AI` nodes — confirmed via the shared Datacenter tree (all three nodes' VMs/storage visible from one session) and `storage.cfg`'s per-storage `nodes` restrictions spanning `homelab`, `AI`, and `cyberrange`.
- **Operator confirmed 22 Sep 2026: intentional, accepted risk**, with specific hardening applied. Verified, not just asserted:
  - **Root SSH trust — corrected 22 Sep 2026, previous framing was wrong.** `ssh AI` does not fail because trust was deliberately cut — it fails because **`ai` has no `/etc/hosts` entry and does not resolve at all** (`ssh -v AI` → "Could not resolve hostname ai: No address associated with hostname"; `/etc/hosts` on the cyberrange host has an entry for `homelab.polaris.home.arpa`/`homelab` but none for `ai`). Whether passwordless cluster SSH trust to the `AI` node exists is therefore **untested, not confirmed absent** — the previous "mitigated" characterization is retracted. `ssh homelab` **does** auto-trust (passwordless root SSH succeeds, confirmed) — this remains a real, live path: a root-level compromise of the cyberrange host currently grants passwordless root SSH to the `homelab` Proxmox node (192.168.10.2). Operator's decision (22 Sep 2026): both `homelab` and `AI` should be reachable by SSH from the cyberrange host, but both should require credentials (key or password), not passwordless trust — not yet implemented, see §3.4.1a below.
  - **Corosync path:** rides on vmbr0 (the trusted/management bridge), not on vmbr1 (the VLAN-aware cyber fabric, which carries no host IP and is not a corosync interface). So no cyberrange **guest** can reach or inject into corosync traffic — exposure is host-to-host on the trusted subnet only, protected by corosync/knet's own pre-shared-key authentication, not by any additional network isolation. The cyberrange host has an unused, inactive spare NIC (`nic0` / `enx309c235f7042`, Active=No, Autostart=No) that could dedicate a private corosync-only link if tighter isolation is ever wanted — noted as a future option, not a current action.
  - **Proxmox RBAC/accounts — now checked:** Datacenter → Permissions → Users lists three accounts beyond the cluster default `root@pam`: `polaris-cyberrange-collector@pve` and `proxcenter@pve` ("Proxcenter service account").
    - **`polaris-cyberrange-collector@pve` — confirmed least-privilege.** Permissions scoped to exactly `/nodes/cyberrange`, `/storage/Cyber_VM`, `/storage/NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange`, `/storage/local`, `/storage/local-lvm`, `/vms/300`, `/vms/301`. Operator-confirmed purpose: automated backups of the cyberrange Proxmox node itself, landing on NAS01 (`/mnt/pve/NAS-AI`, `/mnt/pve/NAS-Configurations` — see §3.4.2). No concern.
    - **`proxcenter@pve` — unresolved, most significant open finding of this audit.** Granted at path `/` (cluster root): near-full Administrator — `Datastore.Allocate`, `Datastore.AllocateSpace`, `Datastore.AllocateTemplate`, `Datastore.Audit`, `Group.Allocate`, `Mapping.Audit`, `Mapping.Use`, `Pool.Allocate`, `Pool.Audit`, `Realm.AllocateUser`, `SDN.Allocate`, `SDN.Audit`, `SDN.Use`, `Sys.Audit`, `Sys.Console`, `Sys.Modify`, `Sys.PowerMgmt`, `Sys.Syslog`, `User.Modify`, `VM.Allocate`, `VM.Audit`, `VM.Config.Disk`, `VM.Config.HWType`, `VM.Config.Memory`, `VM.Config.Network`, `VM.Config.Options`, `VM.Console`, `VM.GuestAgent.Audit`, `VM.GuestAgent.FileRead`, `VM.GuestAgent.FileSystemMgmt`, `VM.GuestAgent.FileWrite`, `VM.GuestAgent.Unrestricted`, `VM.Migrate`, `VM.PowerMgmt`, `VM.Replicate`, `VM.Snapshot`, `VM.Snapshot.Rollback`. **Operator does not recall creating this account** and does not believe it is a Proxmox default; suspects it may relate to automated homelab-wide backups but the grant far exceeds what a backup tool needs. **Operator's explicit decision (22 Sep 2026): will not disable it without first knowing what it was created for** — investigation before action, not action before investigation. Proposed non-destructive investigation path (not yet executed):
      1. GUI: Datacenter → Permissions → API Tokens — check whether `proxcenter@pve` has any active API token(s), and their creation/expiry metadata.
      2. Search the rest of the homelab environment (Docker01's running services/Portainer, n8n workflows and credentials on CT402, Ansible inventory/roles, cron on any host, Homepage dashboard entries) for the literal string "proxcenter" or "Proxcenter" to identify what tool or integration this account name maps to.
      3. If cluster task-log retention still covers it, check for the account-creation event itself (who/when) via the Proxmox task log or `journalctl` on the cluster's current master node.
    - **Purpose identified 22 Sep 2026 (operator-determined): `proxcenter@pve` is the API token credential used for Proxmox homelab-wide backup automation.** The core mystery (unrecognized account) is resolved. One observation carried forward, not yet a decision: the grant is still cluster-root (`/`), near-full-Administrator — materially broader than `polaris-cyberrange-collector@pve`'s tight per-node/per-storage/per-VM scoping for the equivalent job on the cyberrange side, and broader than the "one credential, one narrowly-scoped purpose" pattern the operator's own AI-homelab documentation applies to every other backup credential in that ecosystem. **Open decision for the operator:** accept this as residual risk (same precedent as the `storage-admin` CIFS credential decision above), or pursue scoping it down to what a backup job actually needs. Not yet decided either way.
- **New external dependency logged (not a cyberrange-side action item):** the homelab's separate AI-homelab documentation (uploaded 22 Sep 2026, most recent status dated 2026-09-17) confirms a `root@pam` Proxmox API token named `ansible` exists on CT402 (the homelab's n8n/Ansible orchestration container), created with Privilege Separation unchecked (full root), and — per that project's own tracked "Carried-Forward Open Items" list — remains **deferred and not yet scoped or excluded from the cyberrange node/pool** as of its most recent status. This token is usable by Ansible playbooks triggered through an n8n AI Agent (an LLM with autonomous tool-calling), reached via an SSH-loopback bridge. Nothing currently prevents this token from targeting `/nodes/cyberrange` or `/vms/300`/`/vms/301`. This is not something to fix from the cyberrange side — it is homelab-side infrastructure — but it is tracked here as a watched external risk with direct reach into the cyberrange node, per operator instruction (22 Sep 2026: "log it as a tracked external risk").
- **Design principle, recorded 22 Sep 2026 (operator-stated):** the cyberrange is intended to be treated as its own independent build, and the AI and homelab nodes as their own independent build, **except where intentionally tied together** — today that means: Proxmox cluster membership (including NAS01 as QDevice), the host-level NAS01 backup mount for the cyberrange node's own configuration backups, and the Tailscale break-glass access path. Anything outside that explicit list (e.g. the `root@pam`/"ansible" token above, or any future homelab-side automation) is treated as out-of-boundary reach that should not exist, not as an accepted extension of the intentional ties.
- **Net assessment:** clustering is a deliberate, accepted risk. The AI node is meaningfully isolated from a cyberrange-host compromise (no passwordless SSH); the homelab node is not — see the SSH-trust decision below. `polaris-cyberrange-collector@pve` is confirmed correctly scoped; `proxcenter@pve` is not yet understood and is the top open item from this audit.

**3.4.1a SSH trust — CLOSED 22 Sep 2026: naming gap fixed; passwordless cluster trust accepted as documented risk**

- **Root cause confirmed 22 Sep 2026, via direct diagnostic (`ssh -v AI`, `getent hosts AI`, `/etc/hosts` grep):** `ssh AI` fails at hostname resolution only — `ai` simply has no `/etc/hosts` entry on the cyberrange host (unlike `homelab`, which does: `192.168.10.2 homelab.polaris.home.arpa homelab`). This is a naming gap, not evidence of cut trust — whether passwordless key trust to the AI node exists underneath is still unknown/untested.
- `ssh homelab` succeeds passwordlessly, confirmed — very likely a direct side effect of Proxmox's cluster-wide pmxcfs key sync (`/root/.ssh/authorized_keys` symlinked to `/etc/pve/priv/authorized_keys`), not a separately configured trust relationship.
- **Operator's desired end-state (22 Sep 2026):** both `homelab` and `AI` reachable via SSH from the cyberrange host shell, both requiring credentials (key or password) — not passwordless, not unreachable.
- **Two separate pieces, different risk levels:**
  1. **AI naming gap — APPLIED AND VERIFIED, 22 Sep 2026. CLOSED.**
     ```bash
     echo "192.168.10.124  ai.polaris.home.arpa ai" >> /etc/hosts
     ```
     Operator applied this and confirmed it resolved correctly. `ai` and `homelab` now both resolve consistently on the cyberrange host.
  2. **Passwordless-trust hardening — researched, and accepted as a documented risk. CLOSED 22 Sep 2026, not fixed.** Before deciding, the operator asked for more information, validation, and ramifications, given that breaking cluster functionality was judged a worse outcome than the risk itself. Research findings (Proxmox's own [Cluster Manager documentation](https://pve.proxmox.com/wiki/Cluster_Manager) and forum threads — [mitigating a compromised cluster node](https://forum.proxmox.com/threads/how-to-mitigate-the-impact-of-a-compromised-cluster-node.43498/), [does Proxmox auto-regenerate authorized_keys](https://forum.proxmox.com/threads/does-proxmox-add-authorized_keys-on-its-own-to-root-should-i-be-worried.152563/)):
     - **What actually depends on this shared trust** (per Proxmox's own docs): console/shell proxying between nodes in the GUI, VM/CT migration in "secure" mode (SSH-tunneled), and storage replication. Corosync/knet (already confirmed isolated to vmbr0, §3.4.1) does not depend on it.
     - **Not durably fixable by hand-editing the file.** `/root/.ssh/authorized_keys` on every cluster node is a symlink into the cluster-wide, pmxcfs-replicated `/etc/pve/priv/authorized_keys`. A forum-documented case shows a manually-removed node key silently reappearing after a reboot — Proxmox's own cluster machinery treats this file as managed state and regenerates it, with no obvious signal when that happens.
     - **Proxmox's own development team advises against attempting this.** Responding directly to the "mitigate a compromised cluster node" question, a Proxmox staff member (Fabian) states that cluster nodes "will always need to trust each other," and that root on any one node already exposes the cluster-wide corosync and API ticket keys regardless of SSH trust specifically — restricting SSH trust alone wouldn't meaningfully improve security while risking real breakage. Community consensus in the same thread: the recommended mitigation for this threat model is network isolation of cluster management traffic, not SSH-trust surgery.
     - **Actual exposure reassessed:** this trust only matters if an attacker gets root on the cyberrange Proxmox *host* itself, not a guest VM — cyberrange's guests sit on vmbr1 (VLAN 10 tagged) with no NIC on vmbr0 and cannot reach the host's management plane at all (§3.4.1). The intended cyberrange attack surface (compromising a purposely-vulnerable guest) has no path to this trust regardless; reaching it would require a VM escape, physical/console access, or a credential compromise elsewhere — a materially higher bar than the cyberrange's normal practice scope.
     - **Lower-risk alternative identified, not yet actioned:** the already-noted, non-blocking host-firewall hardening in §3.4.4 (enable `pve-firewall` at the Datacenter level; scope 8006/22/API to Admin01/Kali01 only, instead of the whole `/24`) reduces who can even attempt to reach the cyberrange host's management plane, without touching the cluster's shared trust file or risking cluster breakage. Not part of this decision; available as a future item at the operator's discretion.
     - **Operator's explicit decision, 22 Sep 2026:** accept the passwordless `homelab` (and untested-but-now-moot `AI`, since `ai` was never confirmed to have passwordless trust in the first place — see above) SSH trust as a documented, accepted risk. Rationale given: the risk is very low likelihood, and preserving cluster stability outweighs closing this specific gap, especially now that Proxmox's own team confirms there is no durable, supported way to close it without degrading real cluster functionality. Same treatment/precedent as `proxcenter@pve` and the `storage-admin` credential elsewhere in this audit.

**3.4.4 Host stability incident — systemd wedge, kernel panic, and recovery (23–24 Sep 2026)**

- **Trigger:** while troubleshooting CT 311 creation failures (checksum mismatches on template downloads, `timedatectl` errors during `vzcreate`), diagnosis traced back to the cyberrange Proxmox host's own **PID 1 (systemd) being wedged** — not crashed, not resource-starved (memory/load were normal), but its D-Bus/private-socket listener had stopped accepting new connections. Evidence: `journald` repeatedly failing to send its `WATCHDOG=1` heartbeat ("Transport endpoint is not connected"), `org.freedesktop.systemd1` activation timeouts blocking new SSH/login sessions, and any systemd-mediated command (`systemctl`, `timedatectl`) failing outright. `ps`/`/proc/1/status` showed PID 1 in a normal `S (sleeping)` state (`wchan=do_wait`) — not deadlocked on I/O, just unresponsive to new control requests. No live remediation was available (`systemctl daemon-reexec` hit the identical "Connection refused" instantly, confirming the control channel itself, not just one slow call, was broken).
- **Root cause of the wedge itself: not identified and likely not identifiable after the fact.** Circumstantial link to host uptime: 53 days without a reboot, during which a kernel package update was evidently installed via `apt` but never booted into.
- **Decision to reboot:** made explicitly, not reactively — checked Start-at-boot settings first (VM300 cyber-fw01 and VM301 kali01 both `Yes`; VM310/VM360 `No`, consistent with normal operating pattern), confirmed `sudo reboot` before considering any forced/SysRq-level option.
- **Reboot outcome: kernel panic — "No working init found."** The default/newest installed kernel, `7.0.14-14-pve`, had a broken or incomplete initramfs (most likely from the same unbooted pending update implicated above) and could not mount root/find init at all. This is a distinct, second problem from the systemd wedge — not a data-loss or filesystem-corruption event, a boot-configuration problem.
- **Recovery — GRUB → Advanced options → older kernel, no data loss:** the "Advanced options for Proxmox VE GNU/Linux" GRUB submenu listed four installed kernel versions (`7.0.14-14-pve` [broken], `7.0.14-12-pve`, `7.0.14-5-pve`, `7.0.2-6-pve`), each with a normal and recovery-mode entry. Booted `7.0.14-12-pve` (not recovery mode) directly — succeeded cleanly on the first attempt. Verified post-boot: `systemctl is-system-running` → `running` (wedge fully gone, not just bypassed); `qm list` confirmed VM300 and VM301 both auto-started as expected; VM310/VM360 correctly stayed stopped. **No VM/CT data was lost or corrupted at any point in this incident** — the panic was a boot-time init failure on the host's own OS disk, unrelated to the `Cyber_VM` ZFS pool or any guest disk.
- **Fix applied and verified:** `proxmox-boot-tool kernel pin 7.0.14-12-pve` — the host will now always boot this known-good kernel by default (confirmed via `proxmox-boot-tool kernel list` showing it under "Pinned kernel"), closing the risk of an unattended reboot (power loss, etc.) landing back on the broken `7.0.14-14-pve`.
- **Deferred, not forgotten — operator decision, 24 Sep 2026:** root-causing *why* `7.0.14-14-pve`'s initramfs was broken (and by extension, understanding what actually wedged systemd in the first place, since the timing strongly suggests they're related) is logged as a future, low-priority investigation — not blocking, since the host is now stable and pinned to a verified-good kernel. Revisit if it recurs or when there's a natural lull.
- **Net effect on Gate 8G2:** this incident happened *during* the CT 311 (attack-ai) build attempt and is very likely the actual reason all prior CT 311 creation attempts failed (both the template checksum corruption and the `timedatectl` failures are consistent with a host whose systemd control plane was already degraded at the time). CT 311 creation succeeded immediately once the host was verified healthy post-recovery.

**3.4.2 NAS mounts — cleanup partially executed; one further leftover identified**

- `pvesm status` (Proxmox's own storage layer, which respects the `nodes` restriction) correctly shows only `Cyber_VM`, `NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange`, `local`, and `local-lvm` as **active** on the cyberrange node — `NAS-AI`, `NAS-Backups`, `NAS-CyberRange`, `NAS-ISO-Repo`, `NAS-Infrastructure`, `NAS-Templates`, `NAS-Windows` all show **disabled/N/A** at the PVE-config level, matching intended design (only the ISO repo storage object should be PVE-managed here).
- `findmnt` on the host showed several of those same "disabled" shares **still mounted at the OS level**, read-write, via the shared `storage-admin` CIFS credential. PVE stopped *managing* them once `nodes` excluded cyberrange, but that doesn't retroactively unmount an already-established CIFS session.
- **Cleanup executed 22 Sep 2026** — `umount` run for `NAS-AI`, `NAS-Windows`, `NAS-Infrastructure`, `NAS-Templates`, `NAS-Backups`, `NAS-CyberRange`. Result: `NAS-Windows`, `NAS-Infrastructure`, `NAS-Templates`, `NAS-Backups`, `NAS-CyberRange` successfully unmounted, confirmed via post-run `findmnt`. `NAS-AI` did **not** unmount.
- **`NAS-AI` non-unmount was correct, not a failure — clarified same day:** `NAS-AI` and a distinctly-named `NAS-Configurations` mount (same underlying CIFS source as the `storage.cfg` `NAS-Backups` entry's `/Configurations` subdir) are the actual, intended landing point for the cyberrange node's own automated backups via `polaris-cyberrange-collector` (§3.4.1). These are **not** stale and must **stay mounted** — the earlier "stale/leftover" framing in this section previously applied to them in error and is now corrected. Only `NAS-AI` and `NAS-Configurations` are the legitimate exceptions; everything else that was unmounted above was correctly identified as leftover.
- **Corroborated independently:** the separate AI-homelab documentation's own tracked open-items list (dated 2026-09-17) independently flags "`NAS-CyberRange` Proxmox storage definition (AI-restricted) is unused — candidate for removal," matching this audit's finding for that specific share without either side referencing the other.
- **`NAS-ISO-Repo` (plain, not `-CyberRange`) — identified as leftover, unmounted, and confirmed. CLOSED 22 Sep 2026.** Operator confirmed disposition: `NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange` was deliberately created to give the cyberrange access to its own ISO content while isolating its credential (`polaris-cyberrange-iso-ro`, read-only) from the broader `NAS-ISO-Repo` that AI/homelab use — the cyberrange node should only ever have the `-CyberRange` share mounted, never the plain one. `umount /mnt/pve/NAS-ISO-Repo` executed; post-run `findmnt | grep -i nas` confirms exactly the intended three mounts remain: `NAS-AI` (`//192.168.10.4/Backups`), `NAS-Configurations` (`//192.168.10.4/Backups/Configurations`), and `NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange` (`//192.168.10.4/ISO-Repo`, via the correctly-scoped read-only `polaris-cyberrange-iso-ro` credential) — both `NAS-AI`/`NAS-Configurations` via the shared `storage-admin` credential (see below), nothing else.
- **Storage-admin CIFS credential — operator decision, closed, not an open item.** The broad `storage-admin` credential (used by `NAS-AI`/`NAS-Configurations` among most other NAS-backed storages) remains unscoped for the cyberrange specifically. Operator's explicit reasoning (22 Sep 2026): the credential's current blast radius is low enough, and the restructuring cost of scoping it down disproportionate enough, that the risk is accepted as-is. Do not re-raise this as an action item unless the operator revisits it.
- **This section is now fully resolved.** NAS mount reconciliation for Gate 8G1 step 6 is complete: exactly `NAS-AI`, `NAS-Configurations`, and `NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange` are mounted on the cyberrange node, matching intended design.

**3.4.3 Tailscale on the host — CLOSED 22 Sep 2026: real isolation designed, root-caused through two separate bugs, and verified working with live traffic**

- `tailscaled` runs directly on the cyberrange Proxmox host (Tailscale IP `100.105.224.92`, tailnet device name `cyberrange`, tag `tag:cyberrange` applied, "Expiry disabled") — a standing VPN overlay entirely outside OPNsense's control. Purpose (operator-stated, unchanged): break-glass remote access to the cyberrange Proxmox host if both OPNsense and the other cluster nodes are unreachable. This purpose itself remains an accepted, intentional design choice.
- **What was previously believed:** an ACL had been created on the Tailscale admin console specifically isolating the cyberrange node from other tailnet devices while preserving the operator's own remote admin access.
- **What live evidence actually shows (22 Sep 2026, direct screenshots of the tailnet's Access Controls "General access rules" and Machines list):**
  - The tailnet's access rules consist of exactly two entries, both Tailscale defaults: `All users and devices → All users and devices → All ports and protocols`, and `autogroup:member → autogroup:member → All ports and protocols`.
  - The `cyberrange` device does carry `tag:cyberrange` (confirmed in the Machines list), but **no access rule references that tag anywhere** — a tag with no rule built against it does nothing.
  - Net effect: under the default `autogroup:member → autogroup:member` rule, the cyberrange node is currently reachable from — and can reach — every other device on the operator's tailnet (`acinomed-linux`, `acinomed-windows-laptop`, `ai`, and any other member device) on **all ports and protocols**. This is the opposite of the peer isolation believed to be in place.
  - Operator also directly confirmed **"shields up" (block-incoming-connections) is not in use either** — ruling out the alternate mitigation description found in the AI-homelab documentation's own historical Decisions Log (which recorded shields-up, not an ACL, as of 2026-09-10, with a full ACL redesign explicitly deferred at that time). Neither of the two previously-recorded explanations for how this was hardened reflects the actual current state.
  - Operator's own assessment on seeing this: the rule shown was "what I was given as sufficient" — i.e., this gap traces to an earlier session or review that treated the tailnet's unmodified default rules as if they constituted an isolating policy. It did not.
  - **Confirmed further, 22 Sep 2026 — full policy file captured via the Access Controls → JSON editor tab.** The policy uses Tailscale's `"grants"` syntax (not the older `"acls"` key). Exactly two active grants exist: the stock blanket `{"src":["*"],"dst":["*"],"ip":["*"]}` (preceded by the literal stock comment `// Allow all connections. // Comment this section out if you want to define specific restrictions.`), and `{"src":["autogroup:member"],"dst":["autogroup:member"],"ip":["*"]}`. Everything else in the file (groups, postures, an example tag-scoped grant, tests) is the stock commented-out example content — genuinely untouched. `"tagOwners"` does include `{"tag:cyberrange": ["autogroup:admin"]}`, so the tag itself is validly declared (an actual, if minimal, prior step was taken — just never followed through with a grant that uses it). A separate `"ssh"` stanza exists for Tailscale's own SSH feature (distinct from network-layer grants), currently just the stock `autogroup:member → autogroup:self, check mode` default — not implicated in this finding. The tailnet has never had a custom access-restricting grant applied, at any point.
  - **New symptom found during the control test, 22 Sep 2026 — likely a separate issue from the ACL/peer-visibility problem.** Even under the fully permissive test policy, with `tailscale ping` succeeding (including a direct, non-relayed connection), the Proxmox web GUI (port 8006) still times out from the laptop. This decouples the remaining problem from ACLs/tags entirely: Tailscale-layer reachability is proven; a TCP application-level connection to a specific port is not completing. Leading hypotheses: pveproxy not listening on the Tailscale interface/IP, or a routing/return-path issue on the cyberrange host affecting response traffic back out over the Tailscale tunnel specifically. Diagnostic commands issued (SSH port test from the laptop; `ss -tlnp` and routing table checks on the cyberrange host) — not yet run.
  - **Diagnostic evidence gathered, 22 Sep 2026 — confirms a general TCP-over-Tailscale problem, not app-specific; operator paused further ACL edits pending this.** Operator's explicit instruction: investigate this new symptom before any further ACL changes ("want to bring this up before changing the acl's again in case it points to something else") — honored; no ACL edit proposed since.
    - `ss -tlnp | grep -E ':22|:8006'` on cyberrange: both `sshd` (`0.0.0.0:22`) and `pveproxy` (`*:8006`) correctly bound to all interfaces — **rules out an application/service-binding cause.**
    - `Test-NetConnection -ComputerName 100.105.224.92 -Port 22` from the laptop **also timed out** (`TcpTestSucceeded: False`) — **rules out an 8006/pveproxy-specific cause.** The problem affects every TCP service over the Tailscale tunnel to this one host, not one application.
    - `ip route` / `ip rule` on cyberrange captured: only the standard `192.168.10.0/24` route via `vmbr0`, plus `fwmark 0x80000/0xff0000`-based policy-routing rules (priorities 5210/5230/5250) and a custom table lookup at priority 5270 ("lookup 52"). No explicit `tailscale0`/CGNAT route visible in plain `ip route` — **assessed as likely expected** (Tailscale on Linux typically uses its own policy-routing table rather than a conventional main-table route), not yet confirmed via `ip route show table 52`.
    - Tangential clue raised by operator, assessed and not treated as a likely direct cause: Firefox on the cyberrange host itself crashes repeatedly when attempting to test `127.0.0.1:8006` locally. Assessed as most likely a separate desktop-environment issue (headless-oriented host, GUI browser instability) rather than a cause of a remote TCP timeout — but not fully dismissed; a local `curl` test (below) was proposed as a cleaner, GUI-independent way to check the same thing.
    - **Net read:** Tailscale's own protocol (WireGuard/UDP, used by `tailscale ping`) reaches the host fine; TCP traffic routed through `tailscale0` does not complete a handshake. Narrows the search to routing/return-path or a host-local firewall/filtering rule outside `pve-firewall` (confirmed disabled at Datacenter level per §3.4.4), rather than a Tailscale node-health or ACL problem.
    - **Proposed next diagnostic step (read-only, not yet run):**
      ```bash
      # 1. Does pveproxy actually answer locally at all? (GUI-independent alternative to the Firefox test)
      curl -vk --max-time 5 https://127.0.0.1:8006

      # 2. Tailscale's own routing table (the "lookup 52" the ip rule output referenced)
      ip route show table 52

      # 3. Any non-pve-firewall ruleset that could be dropping this traffic
      iptables -S
      iptables -S -t nat

      # 4. Reverse-path filtering can silently drop return traffic on multi-interface hosts
      sysctl net.ipv4.conf.all.rp_filter net.ipv4.conf.tailscale0.rp_filter net.ipv4.conf.vmbr0.rp_filter
      ```
      If none of these explain it, the decisive follow-up: run `tcpdump -i tailscale0 -n 'tcp port 22 or tcp port 8006'` on cyberrange while simultaneously running `Test-NetConnection -ComputerName 100.105.224.92 -Port 22` from the laptop, to see whether the SYN even arrives at the interface and, if so, whether anything answers it.
    - **Diagnostic batch results, 22 Sep 2026 — reframes the investigation toward `tailscaled`'s own data-plane, not OS routing/firewall.**
      - `curl -vk https://127.0.0.1:8006` on cyberrange: clean `HTTP/1.1 200 OK`, full page returned — **pveproxy itself is completely healthy locally.**
      - `ip route show table 52` (Tailscale's own per-peer routing table): populated normally with a host route to each known peer, including the laptop (`100.110.193.9 dev tailscale0`) — **rules out a routing-table cause.**
      - `iptables -S`: Tailscale's own auto-installed `ts-input` chain has `-i tailscale0 -j ACCEPT` positioned *before* the `100.64.0.0/10 ! -i tailscale0 DROP` rule — any packet arriving on the `tailscale0` interface is accepted by the OS firewall. **Rules out the host's own iptables as the blocker.**
      - `rp_filter`: `= 2` (loose mode) on `all`, `tailscale0`, and `vmbr0` — loose mode does not drop on asymmetric interface paths. **Rules out reverse-path filtering.**
      - **`tcpdump -i tailscale0 -n 'tcp port 22 or tcp port 8006'` on cyberrange, run simultaneously with `Test-NetConnection -Port 22` from the laptop: captured zero packets.** No TCP SYN ever appears on the `tailscale0` interface at all.
      - **Reassessment: `tailscale ping` succeeding does not prove real IP packets reach `tailscale0`.** On Linux, `tailscale ping` primarily exercises Tailscale's own control-plane NAT-traversal/"disco" handshake inside the `tailscaled` process itself — confirming a direct or relayed WireGuard path exists — not necessarily that a real TCP/IP packet gets written to the `tun` device and handed to the kernel. With OS-level routing, OS-level firewall, and the application itself all now confirmed clean, the leading hypothesis is that the block is happening **inside `tailscaled`'s own netstack** (e.g. its internal packet filter derived from the ACL/grants engine), before a packet is ever written to `tailscale0` — which would explain why nothing shows up in a capture on that interface even under the currently-permissive test policy.
      - **Proposed next diagnostic step (read-only, not yet run):**
        1. Plain OS-level `ping 100.105.224.92` (real ICMP, not `tailscale ping`) from the laptop, simultaneous with `tcpdump -i tailscale0 -n icmp` on cyberrange — isolates whether *any* real IP traffic reaches the tun interface, or whether this is protocol/port-specific.
        2. `journalctl -u tailscaled -n 200 --no-pager` on cyberrange immediately after an attempt — checks whether `tailscaled` itself logs a packet-filter/ACL-drop decision that the admin console's "Preview Rules" tool doesn't surface (a mismatch already seen once earlier in this investigation).
      - **ROOT CAUSE FOUND, 22 Sep 2026 — Shields Up is enabled locally on the cyberrange node itself, independent of the tailnet ACL entirely.** `journalctl -u tailscaled` on cyberrange, captured immediately after both an ICMP ping and TCP connection attempts from the laptop, shows two conclusive pieces of evidence:
        1. The daemon's own loaded backend prefs: `pm: using backend prefs for "profile-4400": Prefs{ra=false dns=false want=true shields=true routes=[] statefulFiltering=false nf=on update=on ...}` — **`shields=true`.**
        2. Explicit drop log entries for every protocol/port attempted, all tagged `no rules matched`: `Drop: TCP{100.110.193.9:... > 100.105.224.92:8006}`, `Drop: TCP{100.110.193.9:... > 100.105.224.92:22}`, `Drop: TCP{100.110.193.9:... > 100.105.224.92:443}`, `Drop: TCP{100.110.193.9:... > 100.105.224.92:80}`, `Drop: ICMPv4{100.110.193.9:0 > 100.105.224.92:0}`, `Drop: UDP{100.110.193.9:137 > 100.105.224.92:137}` — the uniform "every protocol, every port, all dropped identically" pattern is Shields Up's signature (it blocks all unsolicited incoming connections at the `tailscaled` data-plane layer, unconditionally, regardless of what the tailnet ACL/grants permit).
        - This fully explains the entire TCP-timeout investigation: `tailscale ping` succeeds because it exercises Tailscale's control-plane handshake, which Shields Up does not filter; the drop happens **inside `tailscaled`'s own netstack**, before a packet is ever written to `tailscale0`, which is why `tcpdump` on that interface captured nothing even though OS-level routing, OS-level firewall (`iptables`), and the application (`pveproxy`) were all independently confirmed healthy. It also holds regardless of the current fully-permissive test ACL, since Shields Up overrides ACL permissions for inbound connections.
        - **Direct correction to this runbook's own prior record:** §3.4.3 previously stated "Operator also directly confirmed 'shields up' ... is not in use either," based on the operator's check at the time. This new evidence — the node's own loaded `tailscaled` preferences — contradicts that and is treated as the more authoritative source (it's the actual running configuration, not a description of it). This also closes an earlier open thread: the AI-homelab documentation's historical Decisions Log (dated 2026-09-10) recorded Shields Up, not an ACL, as the then-current mitigation — that record was accurate and, it now appears, was simply never toggled off when the later ACL-based approach was picked up.
        - **Not yet established:** whether Shields Up also explains the earlier "zero peers" symptom seen during the tag-based `grants`/`acls` attempts (§3.4.3 above). Shields Up affects inbound connection acceptance, not netmap peer visibility, so that earlier symptom may have a separate, still-unresolved cause — flagged to revisit if/when the tag-based isolation rule is reattempted, not assumed solved by this finding.
        - **Per Operating Method rule #12: this is a confirmed misconfiguration, but the correction is a separate, explicitly approved bounded step — not applied in the same breath as this finding.** Proposed command, awaiting approval:
          ```bash
          tailscale set --shields-up=false
          ```
          Operator flagged for consideration before approving: since the tailnet ACL is currently still left in the fully-permissive test state (§3.4.3 above), disabling Shields Up right now would make cyberrange briefly reachable from every tailnet device, not just the intended admin device, until the real tag-based isolation rule is finished and reapplied.
        - **APPLIED AND VERIFIED, 22 Sep 2026 — CLOSED.** Operator's explicit risk assessment before approving: acceptable, since nothing sensitive/harmful is built on the cyberrange yet. `tailscale set --shields-up=false` executed on the cyberrange host. Operator confirmed the Proxmox web GUI now loads correctly from the laptop. **This conclusively confirms Shields Up, not the ACL/tag mechanism, was the actual cause of the TCP-timeout symptom.** The tailnet ACL itself remains in the fully-permissive test state left over from the earlier control test — this is the next item to address (see below), not yet a completed isolation.
  - **Tailscale peer-isolation ACL — final rule applied and verified working. CLOSED 22 Sep 2026.** With Shields Up correctly disabled and the device tags reconfirmed current on the Machines page, the drafted `acls` replacement (below) was applied via the JSON editor and verified with Preview Rules, then tested with real connections rather than `tailscale ping` alone, per the lesson from the shields-up investigation:
    ```json
    "acls": [
        {"action": "accept", "src": ["autogroup:member"], "dst": ["autogroup:member:*"]},
        {"action": "accept", "src": ["tag:cyberrange-admin"], "dst": ["tag:cyberrange:*"]},
        {"action": "accept", "src": ["tag:cyberrange-admin"], "dst": ["autogroup:member:*"]},
        {"action": "accept", "src": ["autogroup:member"], "dst": ["tag:cyberrange-admin:*"]},
    ],
    ```
    - **Result: web GUI access from `acinomed-windows-laptop` (tagged `tag:cyberrange-admin`) confirmed successful.** The intended admin path works.
    - **Result: access attempt from the `homelab` node failed.** This is the correct, intended outcome, not a new problem — `homelab` is untagged (`autogroup:member`), and the rule explicitly denies `autogroup:member → tag:cyberrange` by omission (no rule grants it). Matches the remediation direction decided earlier in this audit ("deny autogroup:member → tag:cyberrange except your own admin device") and the design principle that cyberrange/homelab/AI are independent builds except for explicitly intentional ties — Tailscale break-glass access was always scoped to the operator's own admin device, not to homelab-to-cyberrange automation.
    - **Net assessment: Tailscale peer isolation for the cyberrange node is now correctly implemented and verified with live traffic.** This closes the single largest open item from Gate 8G1 step 6, and the longest diagnostic thread in this runbook's history (v1.9 through this entry) — full chronology preserved above and in the revision history for anyone needing the root-cause trail (stock-default discovery → Tailscale bug #15097 → failed `hosts`-alias attempt → failed tag+`grants` attempt (zero peers, cause still not fully explained — see note below) → `acls` pivot (same symptom) → control-test breakthrough (proved node-level health) → new TCP-timeout symptom → Shields Up identified and fixed via `journalctl` evidence → final tag-based `acls` rule applied and verified with real traffic).
    - **One loose thread, not blocking, carried forward for awareness only:** the earlier "zero peers" symptom seen during the first tag-based `grants`/`acls` attempts (§3.4.3 above, v1.15–v1.16) was never independently explained — Shields Up was active the whole time but, per its documented behavior, only filters inbound connection acceptance, not netmap peer visibility, so it's not a confirmed explanation for that specific earlier symptom. Since the final rule now works correctly end-to-end, this is not being chased further, but is noted in case a similar symptom resurfaces during future ACL changes.
    - **Still separately unconfirmed, carried forward from before:** whether `tag:cyberrange`'s general internet egress (exit-node/`autogroup:internet` routing) is restricted — distinct from peer isolation, not yet built, not currently blocking.
  - **Control test result, 22 Sep 2026 — conclusively rules out a node-level cause.** Under the fully permissive `acls` rule, `cyberrange` immediately appears as a peer on the laptop (`tagged-devices`) and `tailscale ping` succeeds both via DERP relay and **direct** (`192.168.30.101:32716`). The cyberrange host's Tailscale registration, network path, and NAT traversal are all confirmed fine. The problem is entirely in how the tag-based rule computes peer relations — not the node.
  - **Diagnosis progress, 22 Sep 2026 (still open, not closed) — pivoted to `acls`, still unresolved; now isolating whether policy is even the cause.** After the `grants`-based tag rule showed correct in "Preview Rules" but still produced zero peers on the cyberrange host even post-restart, the policy was rewritten using Tailscale's older, long-stable `acls` syntax (same tag-based logic, `action: accept` rules) to rule out `grants`-specific bugs — no change, identical symptom (`tailscale status` on cyberrange still shows zero peers; laptop still gets "no matching peer" / connection timeout). The device detail page for `cyberrange` was also reviewed: no approval banner, valid public/local endpoints, real DERP relay latencies, "Connected" — nothing indicating a node-registration problem visible there. **Operator proposed, and it's the right next test: temporarily revert to the fully permissive stock `acls` rule (`{"action":"accept","src":["*"],"dst":["*:*"]}`) as a control** — if connectivity still fails under a fully open policy, the problem is unrelated to any ACL/tag work and is node-level (Tailscale registration/state on the cyberrange host); if it works, the problem is specifically in how identity/tag-based rules compute peer relations for this destination. Test in progress, result not yet known. This is an explicit diagnostic step, not a decision to abandon isolation — the real rule will be restored once the cause is identified. Root cause of the first failed attempt confirmed: a known, currently-open Tailscale bug ([tailscale/tailscale#15097](https://github.com/tailscale/tailscale/issues/15097)) — the `src` field in both `acls` and `grants` does not honor `hosts` aliases or raw IPs, only identity-based selectors (user/email, group, tag, autogroup). Revised to a tag-based approach: new `tag:cyberrange-admin` declared in `tagOwners`, applied to `acinomed-windows-laptop` via the Machines page, grants rewritten to reference the tag (plus two additional grants restoring the now-tagged laptop's normal two-way access to the rest of the untagged fleet, since tagging removes it from `autogroup:member`). **Tailscale's own "Preview Rules" tool confirms the saved policy is correct** — `tag:cyberrange-admin` is shown with explicit access to `tag:cyberrange:*` and `autogroup:member:*`. Despite this, the cyberrange host's own `tailscale status` still reports **zero peers of any kind** — not the granted relationship, not any other device. A full `systemctl restart tailscaled` on the cyberrange host (low-risk, host-level only, no VM/guest impact) was tried to rule out a stale/stuck daemon state — the daemon reconnected cleanly ("Connected" status, valid Tailscale IP) but still shows zero peers afterward. This rules out both a policy-syntax problem and a stale-daemon-cache problem, pointing instead to something on the control-plane/device-registration side specific to this node. Separately clarified: the operator's observation that `homelab` can no longer reach `cyberrange` via Tailscale is **expected, not a new symptom** — `homelab` is untagged/`autogroup:member` and `cyberrange` is now deliberately isolated from that group by design.
  - **Remediation applied 22 Sep 2026 — unexpected result, under active diagnosis, NOT closed.** The operator applied the drafted edit and tested from `acinomed-windows-laptop` (the intended preserved-access device) against `https://100.105.224.92:8006` (cyberrange's Tailscale IP, Proxmox GUI port) — result: `ERR_CONNECTION_TIMED_OUT`, i.e. the device the rule was supposed to keep *in* also lost access. This means either the edit itself is faulty (syntax/semantics not doing what was intended), or an unrelated cause (e.g. `tailscaled` not actually running on the cyberrange host at test time) is responsible. Diagnostic commands issued for both ends (`tailscale status`/`tailscale ping`/`Test-NetConnection` on the laptop; `tailscale status`/`systemctl status tailscaled` on the cyberrange host), plus a request to confirm the save succeeded cleanly and to re-paste the saved `grants`/`hosts` sections. **No further ACL edit proposed until this is diagnosed.** Not a stop-condition event — Kali01/Admin01 management paths are unaffected; this only concerns the Tailscale break-glass backup path. Remove the blanket allow-all grant; keep the `autogroup:member ↔ autogroup:member` grant (tagged devices, including `tag:cyberrange`, are not part of `autogroup:member`, so they're automatically excluded — no change needed there); add a new `"hosts"` entry naming the operator's admin laptop by its stable Tailscale IP, and a new narrow grant permitting only that named host to reach `tag:cyberrange` on all ports. Net effect: the cyberrange node becomes reachable via Tailscale only from that one device; every other tailnet device (tagged or not) loses reachability to it. **Host alias corrected 22 Sep 2026** (operator caught this before applying): the `"hosts"` key is an arbitrary label in Tailscale's syntax, not required to match the device's real name — but using an unrelated label (`admin-laptop`) would have made the policy needlessly hard to read against the Machines list. Corrected to use the real device name as the alias: `"hosts": {"acinomed-windows-laptop": "100.110.193.9"}`, referenced the same way in the grant's `src`. Exact proposed JSON given to the operator to apply via the JSON editor and verify with the console's own "Preview changes" tool before saving; awaiting the operator's application and confirmation.
- **Remediation direction decided 22 Sep 2026: build a real `tag:cyberrange`-scoped access rule** — deny `autogroup:member` → `tag:cyberrange`, with an explicit exception preserving the operator's own admin device(s). Not yet drafted or applied. Per Operating Method rule #12, exact ACL edit is its own bounded step; before drafting it, two read-only inputs are needed from the operator: (1) the tailnet's current full Access Controls policy (raw JSON, from the Tailscale admin console's Access Controls editor) so the added rule is an exact, additive change rather than a guess at the surrounding file structure, and (2) which device(s) should be the preserved exception (presumably the operator's own laptop/phone from the Machines list — `acinomed-linux` / `acinomed-windows-laptop` / other — needs explicit confirmation, not assumed).
- Still separately unconfirmed, unchanged from the prior version of this section: whether the cyberrange node's Tailscale tag has general internet egress (exit-node/`autogroup:internet` routing) restricted — distinct from peer isolation. Operator previously noted (in an earlier turn, on the general topic of Tailscale ACLs) that internet egress is not currently restricted and that building a policy for it "would not be a bad step... it only helps" — a real, acknowledged future improvement, not yet built, not currently blocking.

**3.4.4 Host firewall and SSH hardening (noted, not blocking)**

- `pve-firewall status` returns `disabled/running` (Datacenter-level firewall enable = No) — no host-level filtering beyond the network itself. The Proxmox GUI (8006), SSH (22), and API are reachable from the **entire** trusted 192.168.10.0/24 subnet, not scoped to just Admin01/Kali01.
- `sshd_config`: `PermitRootLogin yes`; `PasswordAuthentication` is commented out, meaning it runs on the OpenSSH compiled default (effectively still enabled) rather than being explicitly hardened. Root is reachable by password from anywhere on the trusted subnet (and, per §3.4.3, from wherever the operator's own Tailscale access originates).
- `rpcbind` listens on all interfaces (111/tcp) — likely vestigial (no NFS in use; storage is CIFS), minor unnecessary attack surface.
- Not addressed as part of this gate; noted for a future hardening pass, at the operator's discretion.

**3.4.5 Clean results**

- No root crontab; nothing unusual in `/etc/cron.d`, `/etc/cron.daily`, `/etc/cron.hourly`, or `systemctl list-timers` (all standard Debian/PVE maintenance jobs).
- `known_hosts` grep for nas01/docker01/dc01 by IP returned no matches — no recorded SSH trust toward those hosts.
- Bonus capture (not part of the audit scope, incidentally confirmed via OPNsense screenshots): VM300 (cyber-fw01) NIC MACs — WAN (vtnet0) `BC:24:11:91:BD:94`, LAN (vtnet1) `BC:24:11:4A:4FC9`. Recorded here since it wasn't previously in §4/§6.

**Runtime resource policy:** The cyberrange Proxmox host has 31.23 GiB usable RAM (~32 GB installed). Observed baseline with VM300+301+310+360 running is 21.43 GiB (68.61%): ~15.13 GiB guest allocation + ~6.3 GiB Proxmox host/kernel overhead. Supersedes the earlier provisional 24 GiB estimate. Build the full inventory, but run only the subset needed for the current scenario, powering guests on/off as needed rather than designing around a fixed cap.

### 5.3 DNS service chain

1. Cyber guests receive their local OPNsense .1 address as gateway and DNS through DHCP.
2. Unbound listens on port 53 and remains the client-facing resolver.
3. Dnsmasq listens on 127.0.0.1 port 53053 and serves DHCP registrations for LAN and all nine cyber zones, including CYBER_HYBRID/VLAN 70 and CYBER_HYBRID_INTERNAL/VLAN 71 as of 4 Oct 2026 — see §5.1. **Note for the record, caught during the VLAN 71 build:** the per-zone domain (e.g. `hybrid-internal.cyberrange.home.arpa`) is set on the DHCP range's own Domain field, which is distinct from (a) the Dnsmasq Domains tab, which is for forwarding overrides to another upstream resolver, and (b) the Dnsmasq General tab's listening-interfaces list, which controls which interfaces Dnsmasq binds to at all and must separately include every cyber zone regardless of that zone's reachability posture.
4. Unbound forwards all nine cyber subdomains and their nine /24 reverse zones to Dnsmasq at 127.0.0.1:53053, including CYBER_HYBRID's (`hybrid.cyberrange.home.arpa` and `70.10.10.in-addr.arpa`, added 4 Oct 2026) and CYBER_HYBRID_INTERNAL's (`hybrid-internal.cyberrange.home.arpa` and `71.10.10.in-addr.arpa`, also added 4 Oct 2026). Note for the record: this chain is configured under Unbound's **Query Forwarding** tab, not "Domain Overrides" as earlier revisions of this doc loosely called it — same mechanism, correcting the terminology.
5. The existing polaris.home.arpa forward to 127.0.0.1:53053 remains. Do not create an ad.polaris or parent cyberrange.home.arpa forward.
6. Public lookups continue through Unbound's normal upstream resolution path.
7. **Static hostname-to-IP records now live in Unbound Host Overrides, not Dnsmasq — standing convention as of 4 Oct 2026.** Found during the VM370 build: Dnsmasq's static host-record engine — both the `addn-hosts` file mechanism the GUI's Hosts tab generates, and the inline `host-record=` config directive — does not resolve any entry, for any domain, under any circumstance tested, including a brand-new entry under an unrelated non-`home.arpa` test domain and a fresh process following a full cyber-fw01 reboot. Full diagnostic chronology in §11.3.1f. Dnsmasq remains correct and unaffected for DHCP lease assignment. Going forward, a new zone's static hostnames (e.g. `cyber-dmz01` and its subdomain aliases) belong under Services → Unbound DNS → Overrides → Host Overrides, using that form's own Aliases field for multiple hostnames resolving to one IP, rather than the Dnsmasq Hosts tab.

## 6 Proxmox and virtual network configuration

### 6.1 Host bridges

| Bridge | Host address | Purpose | Key controls |
|---|---|---|---|
| vmbr0 | 192.168.10.119/24; gateway 192.168.10.1 | Trusted host management and OPNsense WAN | VM300 net0 only for cyber firewall WAN. Also the host's own management-plane exposure; see §3.3.4 and Gate 8G1. |
| vmbr1 | No host IP | Internal cyber fabric | VLAN-aware; native VLAN 1 plus tags 10,20,30,40,50,60,99 |

### 6.2 OPNsense and guest NIC rules

- VM300 net0 attaches to vmbr0 for the 192.168.10.120/24 WAN.
- VM300 net1 attaches to vmbr1, retains native VLAN 1, and explicitly trunks VLANs 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 71, and 99 (70 added 4 Oct 2026 — see §11.3.1c; 71 added 4 Oct 2026, quoted correctly on the first attempt this time — see §11.3.1e).
- VM301 Kali01 attaches to vmbr1 without a VLAN tag and uses 10.10.1.85/24.
- VM310 belongs on vmbr1 with tag 10 and must have no second NIC or vmbr0 attachment.
- VM360 has exactly one VirtIO NIC on vmbr1 with tag 60 and Proxmox firewall enabled.
- Future workload guests receive one VirtIO NIC on vmbr1 with the VMID-aligned tag. Start at boot remains disabled unless a later gate changes it.
- VM311 and VM351 (LXC) follow the same one-NIC, VLAN-aligned-tag rule as any other guest; GPU device passthrough is a host-level cgroup binding and does not change the network attachment rule.

### 6.3 Gate 8E trunk correction

VM360 initially broadcast DHCP on its tagged NIC but VM300 did not receive the frames because the OPNsense-side Proxmox port admitted only untagged VLAN 1. VM300 net1 was corrected to retain native VLAN 1 while explicitly admitting VLANs 10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, and 99. A clean cold start then obtained DHCP without restarting networking. This validated trunk configuration must not be removed during later guest troubleshooting.

### 6.4 Clone limitation

A full clone from the VM360 baseline-offline snapshot was attempted. Proxmox reported that a full clone was not supported for the Cyber_VM snapshot-backed disk. Do not assume VM360 is a universal clone source. Build later final operating systems from approved installation media or use a separately validated template workflow.

Confirmed 22 Sep 2026: LAN rules allow DHCP/DNS to OPNsense, allow KALI01 HTTPS management, block other LAN sources from OPNsense HTTPS management, block LAN → TRUSTED_INFRASTRUCTURE, block LAN → RFC1918_NETWORKS, allow LAN → !RFC1918_NETWORKS (public only); default allow-LAN-to-any rule is disabled. |
| Tagged interfaces | 10.10.VLAN.1/24 | Guest gateway and approved DNS | Control plane blocked except DNS on active-purpose zones |

### 7.2 DHCP, DNS, and Source NAT

- Dnsmasq is enabled on LAN and all seven cyber interfaces, listens on port 53053, and provides the eight approved DHCP ranges.
- Unbound is enabled on port 53 with network and outgoing interfaces set to All, transparent local-zone behavior, and the approved forwarding table.
- Kea DHCP and ISC DHCP are not the active DHCP services.
- Automatic Dnsmasq service rules remain above manual interface rules. Do not edit automatically generated rules.
- Firewall → NAT → Source NAT remains in Automatic Source NAT rule generation mode. Generated WAN rules translate LAN, loopback, and cyber interface networks to the WAN address.
- No manual outbound NAT rule is required merely to give an active-purpose cyber VLAN Internet access. Quarantine can appear in the generated source set but still has no permitted upstream path.
