Option (a) was rejected because it requires opening a path to/from kali01 — the trusted admin + OPNsense-management host the operator explicitly keeps closed. **Chosen: rebuild as a graphical analyst workstation.** Navigator is then viewed in a browser on the box itself (via the Proxmox console), so the web port never leaves VLAN 60 — no OPNsense rule, no UFW inbound allow, no second trusted-host dependency, and the zone's deliberate one-way isolation (no CYBER_ATTACK exception, §7.4) is fully preserved. This also sets VM360 up as a general analyst box for future on-box tooling (BloodHound GUI, CyberChef, SIEM dashboards).

**Rebuild:**
- Destroyed and recreated VMID 360 (`qm destroy` / `qm create`), keeping hostname `analysisprobe01` and the single NIC on vmbr1 tag 60. Bumped to 1 socket / 4 cores / 8192 MiB / 60 GiB (`Cyber_VM`), SeaBIOS / i440fx / virtio-scsi-pci / virtio NIC, `--vga virtio` for a usable noVNC console. The old `baseline-offline` snapshot was discarded with the old disposable box.
- **Ubuntu Desktop 26.04** (GNOME), user `analyst`, `qemu-guest-agent` installed. Networking left at **DHCP** (per the standing per-zone convention) — leased `10.10.60.141`, DNS `10.10.60.1`, domain `analysis.cyberrange.home.arpa`. Internet-allowed zone, so Docker/npm and the ATT&CK STIX fetch all reach out fine.
- Standard segmentation validation passed: outbound HTTPS `200`; all four protected destinations (`10.10.1.85`, `192.168.10.119`, `10.10.20.10`, `10.10.1.1`) blocked.

**Hardening (VM332 template, with one deliberate deviation):**
- `apt full-upgrade` after the ForceIPv4 fix below; `unattended-upgrades` confirmed; CUPS + avahi disabled/trimmed (same unexpected-surface discipline as VM334/335). Post-trim `ss -tulpn` clean — only loopback `systemd-resolved` (53) and `chrony` (323), nothing bound to `10.10.60.141`.
- **Deviation from the template's SSH-allow:** UFW default-deny incoming / allow outgoing with **no inbound allow at all** (not even 22). Ubuntu Desktop ships no `openssh-server`, and the box is administered via the Proxmox console with Navigator viewed on-box, so there is no remote-admin path by design. Maximally closed, appropriate for an analyst vantage.
- `hardened_baseline` snapshot taken **before** Docker (15:35), preserving a clean pre-content rollback point.

**Content — MITRE ATT&CK Navigator (reference/mapping only):**
- Docker installed; Navigator (v5.3.2) built from the MITRE repo (`mitre-attack/attack-navigator`) and run as a **loopback-only** container: `docker run -d --restart unless-stopped -p 127.0.0.1:4200:80 attack-navigator:local`. Viewed in on-box Firefox at `http://localhost:4200`; the STIX bundle fetches over the zone's Internet pass. `navigator-deployed` snapshot taken after (fs-consistent, agent up). Local-bundle pin for reproducible layers offered as a future option, not done.
- **Scope note:** Navigator is a reference/mapping tool only. The active breach-and-attack-simulation trio (Infection Monkey / Caldera / Atomic Red Team) stays in the SIEM build (VM350) per the 26 Sep decision, reaffirmed by the operator 7 Oct 2026 — the Analysis zone and SIEM zone are kept as distinct "map/track here" vs "run-and-detect there" roles.

**Three reusable findings worth carrying forward:**
1. **ForceIPv4 for heavy-download boxes.** The Desktop `full-upgrade` (linux-firmware, mesa, GNOME, language packs — far more than any prior server box pulled) failed with a flood of IPv6 `Network is unreachable` errors: the range has no IPv6 egress, and apt was resolving AAAA records and attempting IPv6 mirrors first. Fixed with `Acquire::ForceIPv4 "true"` in `/etc/apt/apt.conf.d/99force-ipv4` (now part of this box's hardened baseline). **Recommended as a standard early step for every future Internet-pulling guest in the range.**
2. **docker-group membership needs a new login.** `usermod -aG docker analyst` doesn't apply to the current shell — `newgrp docker` or a re-login is required before non-sudo `docker` works (this caused the initial `permission denied on /var/run/docker.sock`). Note that docker-group membership is **root-equivalent**; accepted here for a disposable box, consistent with the VM370 precedent.
3. **Navigator image serves on container port 80, and the Ubuntu guest-agent needs an `[Install]` override.** The production image runs nginx on port 80 (not the 4200 Angular dev-server port), so it must be published `4200:80`, not `4200:4200` (an initial `4200:4200` mapping produced an empty page). Separately, Ubuntu's `qemu-guest-agent` unit has no `[Install]` section (udev/virtio-activated), so `systemctl enable` warns and it may not be up at an arbitrary moment; a drop-in `[Install] WantedBy=multi-user.target` override plus `enable` makes it persistent, which keeps snapshots fs-consistent (the first `navigator-deployed` snapshot was crash-consistent because the agent wasn't up at that instant; re-taken fs-consistent after the fix).

**11.3.1j Gate 8G3 item 5 — SIEM zone core (VM350 `cyber-siem01`): Wazuh + Infection Monkey + Caldera + Atomic Red Team — CLOSED 8 Oct 2026**

**Scope:** this closes the SIEM *core* — the detection stack and BAS tooling installed, persistent, and serving locally. The cross-zone wiring (VM360→VM350 dashboard access, Wazuh agent telemetry from the other zones, and the Monkey/Caldera/ART lateral-movement passes) is **Gate 8H** (roadmap item 6), the next step — nothing is reachable cross-zone yet, by design. VM351 defense-ai is built after Gate 8H.

**Build:**
- VM350 `cyber-siem01`: **Ubuntu Server 24.04 LTS** (1 socket / 6 cores / 16 GiB / 120 GiB on `Cyber_VM`, SeaBIOS/i440fx/virtio), VLAN 50, DHCP `10.10.50.149`. **24.04 LTS chosen deliberately over the range's usual 26.04** — Wazuh 4.x officially validates 22.04/24.04, not 26.04; the one box whose job is stability shouldn't fight an unsupported-distro packaging matrix (same lesson as the ROCm-on-Debian rebuild, §3.3.5). `--cpu host` set (finding 1). Hardened on the VM332 template (ForceIPv4 drop-in first, `full-upgrade`, UFW default-deny-inbound with **no** inbound allow yet, unattended-upgrades, `vm.max_map_count=262144` for the indexer); `hardened_baseline` snapshot taken **before** Wazuh/Docker.
- **Wazuh single-node** via the native all-in-one installer (indexer + manager + dashboard + filebeat, all systemd services) — dashboard **HTTPS :443** (serve-check 302 → login).
- **Infection Monkey** as Docker (`infectionmonkey/monkey-island` + `mongo:6.0`, host networking by design), Island **HTTPS :5000** (serve-check 200), `--restart unless-stopped` + `systemctl enable docker`.
- **MITRE Caldera 5.3.0** native install under user-owned `/caldera` (venv + Node/npm front-end + Go ≥1.24 sandcat), run as a `caldera.service` systemd unit, web UI **HTTP :8888** (serve-check 200 over http).
- **Atomic Red Team** runner installed (PowerShell 7 + `Invoke-AtomicRedTeam` + atomics) — executes atomics *on targets* via remoting, which is a Gate 8H path; `pip install docker` added so Caldera's optional `builder` plugin loads.
- `siem-deployed` snapshot taken after persistence was configured.

**Credentials:** Caldera ran with `--insecure` → default `red`/`blue` = `admin` (static defaults, not generated/printed); **must be changed before Gate 8H exposes :8888**. Wazuh admin password saved by operator. Monkey Island admin is set on first UI access (pending the Gate 8H dashboard pass).

**Reusable build findings (this zone surfaced a full chain):**
1. **Modern DB/engine containers need `--cpu host`.** MongoDB 6.0 crash-looped with SIGILL (exit 132), logging *"MongoDB 5.0+ requires a CPU with AVX support"* — Proxmox's default CPU type masks AVX. `qm set 350 --cpu host` + a full power-cycle fixed it. Same class as VM370's MySQL `x86-64-v2` crash (§11.3.1g). **`--cpu host` is now standard in the create command for any guest running a modern DB/engine.**
2. **Infection Monkey** image is `infectionmonkey/monkey-island` (one word, no hyphen); host networking is correct/by-design (Monkey must see the network to simulate propagation). Not deprecated — Akamai docs current as of Oct 2026.
3. **Caldera native-build chain.** The Docker build fails (sandcat/gocat `go.mod` needs Go 1.24 + `toolchain`, but the pinned 5.3.0 Dockerfile ships older Go). Native is the reliable path and needs, in order: a **user-owned** clone (don't `sudo git clone` — a root-owned tree gives pip `Errno 13`); **Node/npm** for the magma front-end (package is `nodejs`, not `node.js`); **Go ≥1.24** (snap `go` timed out here — use the official tarball); and **`git submodule update --init --recursive`** after `git checkout 5.3.0`, or plugins like `manx` sit at the wrong commit (`No module named 'plugins.manx.app.c_session'`).
4. **Caldera serves plain HTTP on :8888** (not HTTPS, unlike Wazuh/Monkey) — `curl -k https://…:8888` returns 000; use http. Relevant to the Gate 8H pass (HTTP, not TLS) and to the plan to change the default creds before exposure.
5. **Persistence:** Wazuh = systemd (installer-enabled); Monkey = `docker --restart unless-stopped` + `systemctl enable docker`; Caldera = a `caldera.service` systemd unit running `server.py --insecure` (no `--build`, Go on PATH for on-demand agent compilation). All three return on a VM350 reboot. Minor: the unit's `[Unit]` header was initially mis-read as `[UNIT]` (section ignored) — cosmetic, `Restart=on-failure` covers the boot race regardless.

**11.3.1k Gate 8G3 item 5 (defense-ai) — VM351 `cyber-defense-ai01`: GPU AI appliance (Ollama + Open-WebUI + Wazuh→AI alert pipeline) — CLOSED 11 Oct 2026 (Phase 7)**

Built after Gate 8H as planned. VM351 is a dual-purpose defensive-AI appliance on the SIEM VLAN: an **interactive** lane (chat / paste / file-analysis) and an **automated** lane (AI triage of live Wazuh alerts), both backed by a local GPU-accelerated model stack.

**Base build (unprivileged LXC, GPU-shared):**
- **Unprivileged LXC** (not a VM), `cyber-defense-ai01`, VLAN 50, DHCP `10.10.50.144`, Ubuntu 24.04, 6 cores / 16 GiB / 4 GiB swap / 40 GiB rootfs on `Cyber_VM`, `--features nesting=1,keyctl=1` (nesting for GPU, keyctl for Docker). Unprivileged LXC chosen deliberately so the box **shares** the RX 7700 XT with the host console (and, later, concurrently with VM311 attack-ai) via render-node passthrough — a full VM would need exclusive vfio, which conflicts with the card being the host console (§3.3.6).
- **GPU passthrough** (same cgroup model as VM311, §3.3.5): `pct set 351 -dev0 /dev/dri/renderD129,gid=993 -dev1 /dev/kfd,gid=993` — only the 7700 XT's render node + `/dev/kfd`, assigned to the in-container `render` GID (993); the `ollama` service user is in `render`+`video`. **Device enumeration verified PCI-anchored** (`/dev/dri/by-path/pci-0000:0e:00.0-render → renderD129`).
- **Ollama** (official installer, bundled ROCm libs — no full ROCm stack, same rationale as §3.3.5) with the RDNA3 override `HSA_OVERRIDE_GFX_VERSION=11.0.0` (gfx1101 presents as gfx1100) in a systemd drop-in. Models: **`deepseek-r1:14b`** (the SOC reasoning/analyst model) + **`llama-guard3:8b`** (an I/O content-safety guardrail — *not* a threat analyst; it classifies whether text violates safety categories, used to screen pipeline I/O). GPU-accelerated confirmed: Ollama log shows `found 1 ROCm device … gfx1100 … offloaded 49/49 layers to GPU`, `ollama ps` = `100% GPU`. One model loads to VRAM at a time (14B ≈ 9 GB, 8B ≈ 5.6 GB; both don't co-reside in 12 GB — Ollama swaps on demand).
- **Wazuh agent** installed on VM351 itself (infra monitoring, not a BAS target) and baked into the baseline. **`hardened-baseline`** LXC snapshot taken (OS + agent + Ollama + models, before the app-stack layers). Not a BAS target, so a plain `hardened-baseline` is correct here — no `monitored-baseline` needed.

**GPU coil-whine fix + persistence (host-side, RX 7700 XT):** audible coil whine under load, traced to the instant clock/current ramp. Power-cap alone was a dead end (narrow 198–220 W window). Fix: enable AMD **OverDrive** via `amdgpu.ppfeaturemask=0xffffffff` on the GRUB cmdline (`/etc/default/grub` → `update-grub`), then cap max SCLK to **2300 MHz** and apply a **−50 mV** undervolt (`pp_od_clk_voltage`: `s 1 2300`, `vo -50`, `c`) in `manual` perf mode — quiet **and** stable through full inference. Persisted via a **PCI-anchored** host systemd one-shot `gpu-undervolt.service` (resolves the card by `/sys/bus/pci/devices/0000:0e:00.0` so renderD renumbering can't break it; errors loudly if `ppfeaturemask` ever drops from the cmdline). This is the project's standing fix for the card's coil whine and survives Proxmox updates.

**Lane 1 — interactive (Open-WebUI):** Docker (host-networking) container `ghcr.io/open-webui/open-webui:main` pointed at local Ollama (`OLLAMA_BASE_URL=http://127.0.0.1:11434` — Ollama never listens beyond loopback), serving the chat/paste/file-upload UI on **:8080**. Reached from the VM360 analyst workstation. **File-analysis guardrail:** keep uploads on this box **static/text-only** (scripts, logs, configs, extracted strings/IOCs) — **never detonate live malware here** (this box holds SIEM read creds); binary samples get `strings`/FLOSS/CAPA first, feed the text. Detonation belongs in a future disposable sandbox zone (same spirit as the deferred Sliver C2 box, §11.3.1h).

**Lane 2 — automated (Wazuh → AI alert pipeline):** a Python poller on VM351 (`/opt/ai-pipeline/poller.py`, systemd `ai-poller.timer` every 5 min) pulls new alerts (`rule.level ≥ 10`) from the Wazuh **Indexer** (`wazuh-alerts-*` on :9200), sends each to `deepseek-r1:14b` with a SOC-analyst prompt, and writes a structured analysis (severity / summary / analysis / recommended-actions) into a custom **`ai-analysis-*`** index, viewed as a Wazuh dashboard from VM360. Cursor+dedupe state file; `--init-now` primes the cursor to "now" so it skips the ~8,900-alert historical backlog; `num_ctx`/`num_predict` bounded and the alert prompt trimmed so a run can't run away. Proven end-to-end (real rootcheck alert → `Critical` analysis indexed, ~8–90 s/alert on GPU).
- **Least-privilege indexer access:** a scoped internal user **`ai-pipeline`** (role `ai_pipeline`: **read** on `wazuh-alerts-*`, **crud+create_index** on `ai-analysis-*`, nothing else) — negative-tested (write to `wazuh-alerts` → 403). Its password was **rotated 11 Oct 2026** after being on-screen during troubleshooting. Creds live in `/opt/ai-pipeline/config.env` (`chmod 600`).
- **`ai-analysis` index template** created (admin, one-time) mapping the analysis fields (`@timestamp`, `alert_id`, `agent_name/ip`, `rule_*`, `mitre_ids`, `ai_severity/summary/analysis`, `source_full` stored-not-indexed).

**TLS correctness (indexer cert reissued):** two defects surfaced and were fixed properly rather than papered over with `-k`. (1) The Wazuh indexer was bound **loopback-only** (`network.host: 127.0.0.1`) so VM351 got connection-refused on :9200 — changed to `0.0.0.0` (safe: single-node via `cluster.initial_master_nodes`, `vm.max_map_count=262144`; loopback stays bound so the dashboard is unaffected). (2) The node cert's SAN listed **only `127.0.0.1`**, so remote TLS verification by IP couldn't pass. **Reissued `wazuh-indexer.pem`** surgically: `openssl x509 -x509toreq` to preserve the exact subject DN (so the security plugin's `nodes_dn` still matches), signed by the **existing root CA** (so admin/dashboard/filebeat certs and VM351's copied `root-ca.pem` all stay valid), with the SAN expanded to add `10.10.50.149` + DNS names (strictly additive). The CA private key — deliberately not kept on the node — was pulled from the install bundle, used to sign, and `shred`-ed afterward. Backup taken first; verified (`openssl verify … OK`, SAN present) before install. Pipeline now connects with full `verify_certs=True` + `ca_certs`, no hostname exception.

**Firewall posture (the appliance adds exactly ONE new cross-zone hole):** VM351 and VM350 are both on VLAN 50, so the Wazuh agent (1514/1515) **and** the alert pull (9200) are **intra-VLAN L2 — no OPNsense rule**, just standing VM350 UFW allows from `10.10.50.0/24`. The **only** new cross-zone OPNsense rule is the UI pass **CYBER_ANALYSIS (VM360 `10.10.60.141`) → VM351:8080** (aliases `DEFENSE_AI`=`10.10.50.144`, `AI_WEBUI_PORT`=`8080`), an exact mirror of the Gate 8H.1 dashboard pass, plus a matching VM351 UFW allow from `10.10.60.0/24`. HTTP (not TLS) on that internal mgmt hop — acceptable for the lab; `ENABLE_SIGNUP` to be flipped off after the admin account is made.

**Boot-robustness (the GPU boot race, fixed):** after the host reboot for the OverDrive change, Ollama first-loaded the model on **CPU** (a boot-time race — GPU not ready when Ollama did its first load), which caused a 10-minute pipeline timeout; a plain `systemctl restart ollama` recovered it to GPU. Root-caused and hardened with a self-healing oneshot **`ollama-gpu-check.service`** (After=ollama) that warms the model post-boot and restarts Ollama if it landed on CPU. The transient "coil whine returned" observation was a red herring — it was **CPU/PSU whine under the CPU-bound poller run**, not a GPU-undervolt failure (the undervolt readout was correct the whole time).

**Known refinement (not blocking):** `deepseek-r1` **over-calls false positives** — it flagged a benign Wazuh rootcheck anomaly on a Docker overlayfs `zoneinfo` file (`hidden from stats but visible in readdir`, a classic overlayfs FP) as a "Critical kernel-level rootkit." Appropriately cautious but noisy; a prompt-tuning pass (feed known-FP patterns / Docker-overlayfs context) is a future improvement. Good illustration that the AI augments, not replaces, the analyst.

**11.3.1a cyber-DC01 (VM320) build and initial validation (27-28 Sep 2026)**

- **Built 27 Sep 2026:** Windows Server 2025 (evaluation media), UEFI/OVMF, q35 machine type, vTPM 2.0, 4 cores, 8 GiB RAM, 80 GiB disk on `Cyber_VM`, single VirtIO NIC on vmbr1 tag 20 (VLAN 20 CYBER_WINDOWS). Static IP `10.10.20.10/24`, gateway/DNS `10.10.20.1` initially, per the zone's DHCP/DNS posture (§5.1-5.3).
- **Promoted to Active Directory Domain Services**, forest/domain `corp.cyberrange.local`. Post-promotion, DNS now points to itself (`::1`/`127.0.0.1`), which is expected and correct for a first DC in a new forest.
- **Validation run 28 Sep 2026 (`ipconfig /all`, `dcdiag`):**
  - `ipconfig /all` confirms the static IP held (`10.10.20.10`) and DNS is self-hosted post-promotion, as expected.
  - Every structural `dcdiag` test passed: CrossRefValidation, CheckSDRefDom, LocatorCheck, Intersite, ForestDnsZones, VerifyReferences. `nslookup corp.cyberrange.local` resolves cleanly via its own DNS.
  - `SystemLog` and `DFSREvent` dcdiag tests failed, but this is expected noise (these two tests flag any warning/error-level event-log entry with little relevance filtering) rather than a real problem, especially on a freshly promoted DC — not tracked as a finding.
  - **Five real findings surfaced, none blocking, none yet remediated:**
    1. **No external NTP source configured for the PDC emulator.** Standard for the first DC in a new forest (no parent domain to sync from), but worth deciding deliberately — directly relevant to the planned Kerberoasting/TGT-attack practice for this zone (§11.3.1), since Kerberos allows only a 5-minute default clock-skew tolerance. Candidate fix: point at OPNsense's NTP service, or an external pool if VLAN 20's Internet-allowed policy covers NTP (UDP 123) through the zone's existing Internet-pass rule (§7.4) — needs confirming, not yet done. **Proposed as the next quick, high-value hardening item, pending operator approval and the baseline snapshot below.**
    2. **RID allocator error** (one-time, event `0x00004102`), most likely a transient promotion-time race rather than a persisting fault. Not yet sanity-checked (e.g. by creating a test AD user) to confirm it isn't recurring.
    3. **WinRM not listening** — expected on a fresh install; only matters if PowerShell remoting into the DC is wanted. Low priority, easy to enable later.
    4. **Secure Boot certificates updated but not yet applied to firmware** — ties to the UEFI/Secure Boot configuration already set on this guest. Low urgency in a lab context, but real and worth closing during the hardening pass.
    5. Repeated DCOM CLSID "Local Activation" warnings in the event log — assessed as near-universally benign/cosmetic on a fresh Windows Server install; not tracked as an action item.
  - **Fits the zone's stated build philosophy exactly (§11.3.1):** clean baseline → snapshot → identify/harden default-config weaknesses → snapshot hardened baseline → deliberate vulnerabilities introduced later. These five items are the "identify default-config weaknesses" step for cyber-DC01.
- **Clean-baseline Proxmox snapshot for cyber-DC01 — CONFIRMED 28 Sep 2026.** Taken per the project's established clean→snapshot→harden→snapshot pattern (used for Kali02, VM360, VM310), consistent with Operating Method rule #4.
- **Sequencing decided, 28 Sep 2026 (operator):** the NTP fix, RID-allocator sanity check, WinRM enablement, and Secure Boot firmware sync are intentionally deferred rather than done now. Operator's stated plan: build VM321 (Windows 11) and VM322 (Windows 10) next, domain-join both, then come back and do the DC01 hardening pass, DC01 configuration, and AD content population (realistic objects — nested groups, SPN'd service accounts, AS-REP-disabled accounts, messy ACLs, cached admin sessions, per §11.3.1's design intent) all together in one pass, once there is a real multi-box domain to configure against rather than a single empty DC. Rationale (implicit in the request): hardening and content population are more efficient to do once, against the finished 3-box topology, than twice. **VM321/VM322 build is the active step — see §11.3.2.**

**11.3.2 VM321/VM322 — Windows AD domain-joined clients (active step, 28 Sep 2026)**

Purpose: complete the 3-box AD lab (§11.3.1) with VM321 (Windows 11) and VM322 (Windows 10), domain-joined to `corp.cyberrange.local`, giving the BloodHound/Kerberoasting/ticket-attack practice goal a realistic small domain to work against. DC01 hardening and AD content population are intentionally deferred to a combined pass after both clients exist (see §11.3.1a's updated status).

**Design decisions carried into this build:**

- **VMIDs 321/322**, within the existing 320-329 VLAN 20 family (§5.4). VM321 = Windows 11, VM322 = Windows 10, per the roadmap (§11.3).
- **Same clean→snapshot pattern as DC01, with two snapshot points per client**, not one: a pre-domain-join baseline (rollback point if the join goes wrong) and a post-domain-join baseline. Domain join is a significant identity change and worth its own recovery point, separate from the plain-OS-install snapshot.
- **Naming convention carries forward (§11.2.1):** Proxmox display name deterministic (host-type + sequence, e.g. matching the existing `Kali02`/`Parrot01` pattern), guest hostname ad hoc and non-matching.
- **Network:** single VirtIO NIC on vmbr1 tag 20, address via the existing DHCP pool (10.10.20.100-199) — same pattern as VM310/VM360, no static IP needed for domain clients.
- **DNS — recommended approach, not yet applied:** point each client's DNS server explicitly at DC01 (`10.10.20.10`) rather than the zone's default `10.10.20.1` (OPNsense/Unbound), since only DC01 hosts the AD-integrated DNS zone with the SRV records domain join and Kerberos depend on. This is a **per-host NIC setting**, not a change to OPNsense or the DNS chain in §5.3 — Operating Method rule #10 (DNS Registry changes need explicit approval) is not triggered by this option. For public/Internet name resolution to keep working from domain-joined clients, DC01's own DNS Server role needs a forwarder configured to `10.10.20.1`. **Alternative, not chosen:** an Unbound domain override forwarding `corp.cyberrange.local` to DC01, which would keep every VLAN 20 client uniformly pointed at `10.10.20.1` (closer to the existing §5.3 pattern) at the cost of an actual OPNsense/DNS-Registry change requiring separate sign-off under rule #10. Flagged here for the record; revisit if the per-host approach proves awkward at scale.
- **UEFI/vTPM:** Windows 11 requires UEFI + Secure Boot + TPM 2.0 to install without a bypass — VM321 needs OVMF/q35/vTPM 2.0, same as DC01. Windows 10 has no such requirement; built UEFI/q35 for consistency with the rest of the AD zone rather than SeaBIOS/i440fx (VM322's call, either works).

**Procedure (repeat per VM):**

1. Proxmox: create VM (VMID 321 or 322) — OVMF/q35, vTPM 2.0 (required for VM321, recommended for VM322), 2-4 cores, 4-8 GiB RAM (Windows 11 wants 4 GiB minimum, 8 GiB comfortable given current host headroom; Windows 10 is lighter, 2-4 GiB is fine), 60-80 GiB disk on `Cyber_VM`, single VirtIO NIC on vmbr1 tag 20.
2. Attach the Windows 11 / Windows 10 install media from `NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange` — source directly from Microsoft, verify the hash, upload via the NAS's own GUI, same sourcing discipline as DC01/Kali02 (§11.2.0), not through the cyberrange host's read-only mount.
3. Install clean; set a local administrator account; assign the ad hoc guest hostname.
4. Install VirtIO drivers and QEMU Guest Agent; confirm Proxmox Options shows Guest Agent = Enabled and `qm agent <vmid> ping` succeeds — same validation pattern as Kali02/Parrot01/DC01.
5. Confirm the DHCP-assigned `10.10.20.x` address; set the client's DNS server to `10.10.20.10` per the DNS decision above.
6. **Snapshot — clean, pre-domain-join baseline.**
7. Domain-join to `corp.cyberrange.local` using a domain admin credential; reboot; confirm via `whoami /fqdn` (or Settings → Account) that the machine reports domain-joined.
8. Confirm the new computer object appears in AD Users and Computers on DC01 (default `Computers` container).
9. **Snapshot — post-domain-join baseline.**

**Validation:** `nslookup corp.cyberrange.local` resolves via DC01 from each client; QEMU Guest Agent responds from Proxmox for both; each client's AD computer object is visible on DC01. Domain-user login is not testable yet (no domain user accounts exist) — that's part of the deferred content-population pass.

**Stop condition:** either client obtains an address or route outside the `10.10.20.0/24` DHCP pool, or shows unexpected reachability outside VLAN 20/CYBER_WINDOWS.

**Status, 29 Sep 2026:**
- **VM321 (Windows 11) — domain join and login fully validated.** Built as Windows 11 Pro (standard Pro, not Pro for Workstations — no functional benefit for this VM's specs, see operator discussion). Baseline pre-domain-join snapshot taken (slightly ahead of the DNS-set step in the procedure above, immaterial — still a valid rollback point). Domain-joined to `corp.cyberrange.local`; confirmed two ways: the AD computer object (`CYBER-SVC`) appears under DC01's default `Computers` container, and an actual domain login as `CORP\Administrator` (the built-in domain admin, created automatically at promotion) succeeded, with `whoami /fqdn` correctly returning `CN=Administrator,CN=Users,DC=corp,DC=cyberrange,DC=local`. An earlier `whoami /fqdn` failure while still logged into the local `cyber` account was a red herring — that command requires an active domain session, not proof of a failed join; the AD computer object was already sufficient evidence the join had succeeded.
  - **Naming note:** the guest's computer name landed as `CYBER-SVC`, close to the planned domain service-account name `cyber_svc` (underscore, to be created in the later content-population pass). Operator is aware and not concerned for now; the local Windows account name will be addressed later too. Flagged here only so the near-duplicate isn't mistaken for an error when the real `cyber_svc` account shows up in AD later.
  - **QEMU Guest Agent — CONFIRMED running, 29 Sep 2026.**
- **VM322 (Windows 10) — CLOSED 29 Sep 2026.** The NAS-ISO-Repo upload blocker was resolved by abandoning browser-based upload entirely: the Windows 10 media (already downloaded to the homelab Proxmox node's local disk) was copied directly into the NAS `ISO-Repo` share via a shell `cp` into the storage's existing host-side mount, bypassing both the NAS's own web GUI (first attempt, failed) and Proxmox's own ISO-upload feature (second attempt, also failed — same generic transport error, this time failing immediately rather than partway, through a different service on a different node, which is what pointed away from a network/browser-tab cause and toward avoiding browser upload for multi-GB files altogether). VM322 built, domain-joined, snapshotted, and QEMU Guest Agent confirmed running — same full validation as VM321.
- **VM321 and VM322 are both fully built, domain-joined, snapshotted, and validated as of 29 Sep 2026. The 3-box AD lab (§11.3.1) is complete.**

**Next — the deferred combined pass is now active:** DC01 hardening (§11.3.1a's five findings: NTP, RID-allocator check, WinRM, Secure Boot firmware sync, plus the DCOM warnings already dispositioned as benign), DC01 configuration, and AD content population (realistic objects per §11.3.1's design intent — nested group memberships, SPN'd service accounts for Kerberoasting, an AS-REP-roastable account or two, a messy ACL, cached admin sessions on a client) all together, since content population needs VM321/VM322 to exist as somewhere for "cached admin sessions" and realistic group structure to actually live.

**11.3.3 Combined DC01 hardening/configuration/content-population pass — execution log**

Working through §11.3.1a's five findings in order (NTP → RID-allocator check → WinRM → Secure Boot firmware sync → AD content population), per operator direction.

**Item 1, NTP — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** Applied, lost to a snapshot revert, then reapplied and confirmed working post-revert.
- Applied 29-30 Sep 2026: `w32tm /config /manualpeerlist:"time.windows.com,0x9 pool.ntp.org,0x9" /syncfromflags:manual /reliable:yes /update`, then `Restart-Service w32time` (not `w32tm` — the command-line tool and the actual service name differ, a one-time mix-up, noted for anyone following this runbook later) and `w32tm /resync /force`. Validated working: `w32tm /query /status` showed `Source: time.windows.com,0x9`, Stratum 5, successful sync.
- **Incident, 30 Sep 2026:** after this config change and a subsequent DC01 reboot, DC01 became unable to authenticate any logon — domain login (`CORP\Administrator`) failed with "the security database on the server does not have a computer account for this workstation trust relationship," and a local-style logon attempt failed with "an attempt was made to logon, but the network logon service was not started." **Operator also attempted Safe Mode and could not log in there either** — a more significant data point than it first appears, since Safe Mode's DSRM local-admin logon path doesn't depend on Netlogon, so a plain "Netlogon/NTDS failed to start" explanation doesn't fully account for the symptom; whatever actually broke was more fundamental than one service failing to start. **Operator resolved it by reverting DC01 to the pre-hardening-pass Proxmox snapshot** (the baseline taken 28 Sep 2026, before any of these five items were started). This fixed access but also rolled back the NTP configuration above and erased the 30 Sep event-log evidence that would have shown the actual root cause — that root cause is therefore **not confirmed and not confirmable after the fact**, the same posture the project has already accepted once before for an unrelated host-level incident (§3.4.4's systemd wedge). Operator's explicit call: not worth pursuing further. Reasonable trade-off given DC01 is disposable/rebuildable by design and the snapshot existed for exactly this purpose.
- **Real finding surfaced by this incident, independent of the lockout itself:** the Directory Service event log (captured fresh after the revert, during item 2's work below) shows a **separate, earlier** snapshot-revert event from 28 Sep 2026, in which Active Directory Domain Services correctly detected the VM-GenerationID change from that revert and ran its designed self-healing sequence — new invocationID issued, RID pool invalidated, SYSVOL/DFSR non-authoritatively resynced (events 2181/2204/2170/2185/2208/2187, timestamped 28 Sep 7:57 PM). This is expected, by-design Windows Server behavior for virtualized DCs (the VM-GenerationID mechanism exists specifically to make hypervisor-snapshot-based DC recovery safe against USN rollback corruption), not an error — genuinely good news for this project's snapshot-heavy operating pattern, since it confirms DC01 self-heals correctly across a revert rather than silently corrupting.
- **Reapplied and confirmed working, 1 Oct 2026.** NTP config reapplied on the post-revert DC01 using the same commands above; operator confirmed it completed successfully with no recurrence of the lockout. **Item 1 closed.**

**Item 2, RID-allocator sanity check — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.**
- Re-run fresh on the post-revert DC01 (the original pre-revert run's results no longer apply, same as item 1). `Get-ADDomain` confirmed DC01 holds RIDMaster/PDCEmulator/InfrastructureMaster (expected, sole DC). `New-ADUser ridtest01` (disabled test account) succeeded; `Get-ADUser ridtest01 -Properties SID` returned a normal, non-reserved RID (`S-1-5-21-840204603-3937290519-3535005189-1601`). `Get-WinEvent -LogName "Directory Service"` scan showed only expected background activity (online defragmentation, the 28 Sep revert self-healing sequence described above) — no new RID-allocator error, no recurrence of the original one-time `dcdiag`-era finding. **Confirmed not a persisting issue**, consistent with the original finding's own assessment that it was most likely a transient promotion-time race. Operator should confirm `Remove-ADUser ridtest01 -Confirm:$false` was run to clean up the test object before moving on, if not already done.

**Item 3, WinRM — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** Enabled on DC01 via `Enable-PSRemoting -Force`; validated locally with `Test-WSMan localhost` (returned a clean response). Cross-zone PowerShell remoting into DC01 (e.g. from kali01 or another VLAN) remains intentionally blocked by current OPNsense policy — that is expected and correct, not a gap; narrow, scoped remoting rules for specific scenarios are deferred to Gate 8H (see §11.5/§11.5.1) rather than opened broadly now.

**Item 4, Secure Boot firmware sync — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.**
- Pre-check: `Get-ItemProperty HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\SecureBoot` showed `AvailableUpdates: 0`, `UEFICA2023Status`/`UEFICA2023Error` both empty (no value), and the `\Microsoft\Windows\PI\Secure-Boot-Update` scheduled task in `Ready` state — read as "not yet attempted," not "already current," since a completed run should populate a status value.
- Fresh DC01 snapshot taken before any change, given Microsoft's own documented risk that some firmware implementations overwrite rather than append DB certificates during this update, which can cause boot failure. DC01 runs OVMF (virtual UEFI); Microsoft's guidance is written for physical/Hyper-V firmware and doesn't explicitly cover this case, so the extra precaution was warranted.
- Task triggered manually (`Start-ScheduledTask -TaskName "Secure-Boot-Update" -TaskPath "\Microsoft\Windows\PI\"`), DC01 rebooted. Post-reboot: registry values unchanged (`AvailableUpdates: 0`, status/error still empty); the `Microsoft-Windows-Secure-Boot-Certificates` event provider does not exist on this host at all (`Get-WinEvent` returned `NoMatchingProvidersFound`); a broad System-log scan for the documented success/failure event IDs (1032/1036/1043/1044/1045/1795/1796/1797/1799/1802/1803) returned zero matches. `Get-ScheduledTaskInfo` confirmed the task actually ran at the expected time with `LastTaskResult: 0` (success, no error). `Confirm-SecureBootUEFI` returned `True` — Secure Boot itself is intact and functioning normally post-reboot; no boot impact occurred.
- **Assessment:** task executed cleanly and made no observable change. Most likely explanation: DC01 was built from a Windows Server 2025 image recent enough (2026) to already include the 2023 Secure Boot CA in its firmware DB baseline, so there was nothing for the update to add — consistent with `AvailableUpdates: 0` meaning "nothing pending" rather than "not yet evaluated." (A secondary, less likely possibility: the underlying firmware-update component simply isn't implemented in OVMF/QEMU's virtual UEFI, which was the gap flagged going into this item.) Not pursued further by parsing the raw UEFI `db` variable to confirm cert contents directly — effort not justified given Secure Boot is confirmed functional and the task completed without error. **Closed as assessed/benign**, not as a confirmed no-op; worth re-checking if a future OS/firmware change surfaces a related issue.

**Items 1-4 are all closed. Item 5 (AD content population) — in progress, broken into sub-steps 5a-5g, tracked below.**

**Item 5, AD content population — execution log**

Design intent per §11.3.1: realistic objects for Kerberoasting/BloodHound/lateral-movement practice, built as a standard OU tree with a deliberately indirect privilege-escalation path through nested group membership. Operator chose realistic fictional names over placeholders (`user1`/`svc1` style) since this is a private lab; "Polaris" used as the fictional top-level OU/company name, after the project itself — cosmetic, trivially renameable.

**5a, OU structure — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** Standard-shape OU tree created under `DC=corp,DC=cyberrange,DC=local`:
```
OU=Polaris
├── OU=Admin Accounts        (Tier 0, reserved — not yet populated)
├── OU=Service Accounts
├── OU=Departments
│   ├── OU=IT
│   ├── OU=Finance
│   ├── OU=HR
│   └── OU=Sales
├── OU=Groups
├── OU=Workstations
└── OU=Servers
```
VM321 (`cyber-svc`) and VM322 (`DESKTOP-R7PF66O`, rebuilt after the DC01 snapshot-revert incident and rejoined — see below) computer objects moved from the default `Computers` container into `OU=Workstations`. Confirmed via `Get-ADComputer -Filter * -SearchBase "OU=Workstations,OU=Polaris,DC=corp,DC=cyberrange,DC=local"`.
- **Note:** `Move-ADObject` failed once from PowerShell with "the object's parent is either uninstantiated or deleted" — a known `ActiveDirectory` module quirk where the cached directory-server connection doesn't immediately see an OU created moments earlier in the same session, even though it exists (confirmed by a concurrent `Get-ADOrganizationalUnit` listing). Worked around via drag-and-drop in ADUC; a fresh PowerShell session would also have cleared it. Not a real defect, just noted for anyone hitting it again.
- **Incidental finding, rebuilding VM322:** after the DC01 snapshot-revert incident (Item 1), VM322 needed to be rebuilt and rejoined to the domain. The rejoin initially failed with "That domain couldn't be found" — root cause was DNS, not the domain/trust itself: the client's DNS server was still whatever OPNsense's DHCP handed out, not DC01, so it couldn't resolve the domain's SRV records. Fixed with `Set-DnsClientServerAddress -ServerAddresses 10.10.20.10` before retrying the join. This is the same manual DNS-pointing step done for VM321 originally; worth calling out explicitly in any future VM321/322-class build procedure so it isn't missed again on a rebuild.

**5b, nested groups — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** Security groups created under `OU=Groups`: flat department-membership groups (`IT-Staff`, `Finance-Staff`, `HR-Staff`, `Sales-Staff`) plus a deliberate privilege-escalation chain — `Help-Desk` → member of → `IT-Admins` → member of → `Server-Admins`. `Server-Admins` intentionally left without real permissions for now; actual elevated AD rights get wired onto it in 5f (messy ACL), so the escalation path has a real payoff once that step lands. Confirmed via ADUC membership screenshots on `IT-Admins` and `Server-Admins`.

**5c, department users — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** Six fictional users created, one per department except IT (two): `mchen`/Marcus Chen and `ppatel`/Priya Patel (IT), `dbrooks`/Daniel Brooks and `lkim`/Laura Kim (Finance), `rnguyen`/Rachel Nguyen (HR), `twalsh`/Tom Walsh (Sales). Each added to their department's `-Staff` group; `mchen` additionally added to `Help-Desk` to complete the escalation-path demo. **Validated end-to-end:** `Get-ADGroupMember -Identity "Server-Admins" -Recursive` returned empty before `mchen` existed (correct — recursive lookup only surfaces real user/computer leaves, not intermediate groups, so no users yet meant nothing to show) and returned `Marcus Chen` once he was added to `Help-Desk` — confirming the two-hop nested-membership path resolves exactly as designed, with nothing on his own account suggesting elevated access.

**5d, Kerberoastable service account — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** `cyber_svc` created for real in `OU=Service Accounts` (the earlier near-duplicate hostname `CYBER-SVC` on VM321 was just a cosmetic naming coincidence, unrelated to this account). `PasswordNeverExpires: $true` set deliberately (realistic service-account misconfiguration). SPN set: `MSSQLSvc/cyber-sql01.corp.cyberrange.local:1433` (target host doesn't need to exist for Kerberoasting purposes). `msDS-SupportedEncryptionTypes` set to `28` (RC4 + AES128 + AES256) — operator's deliberate choice over RC4-only or AES-only, to reflect a realistic mixed-encryption environment rather than an artificially easy or artificially hardened single-mode target; RC4 remaining available still permits the classic easy-crack Kerberoasting case. Confirmed via `Get-ADUser -Properties ServicePrincipalNames, PasswordNeverExpires, msDS-SupportedEncryptionTypes`. Actually attempting a Kerberoasting attack against this account is left for a future attack-side exercise, not part of this build step.

**Parking lot, 1 Oct 2026 — SeImpersonatePrivilege / "Potato" escalation chain, not in scope for this build pass.** Operator raised, then explicitly deferred: the JuicyPotato/RoguePotato/PrintSpoofer family of local-privilege-escalation techniques, which abuse a service account holding `SeImpersonatePrivilege` (and sometimes `SeAssignPrimaryTokenPrivilege`) to coerce a privileged token and escalate to SYSTEM. Requires a specific service actually running under a specific account with those specific rights — scenario-content, not foundation-content, same category as other "future scenario-based accounts" operator is deliberately setting aside for now rather than letting scope creep into the base-infrastructure build. **Not built, not decided on placement** (which host, which service) — flagged here purely so it isn't lost and doesn't need re-deriving when scenario-building work starts.

**5e, AS-REP roastable account — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** `jthompson` (James Thompson) created in `OU=IT`, framed as a forgotten/legacy contractor account (realistic justification for pre-auth being disabled and never revisited). `Set-ADAccountControl -DoesNotRequirePreAuth $true` applied, confirmed via `Get-ADUser -Properties DoesNotRequirePreAuth` returning `True`. Added to `IT-Staff` for realistic department presence. Deliberately a separate account from `cyber_svc` so AS-REP roasting and Kerberoasting remain two distinct, independently discoverable findings rather than overlapping on one object.

**5f, messy ACL — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** `GenericAll` granted to `Server-Admins` on `OU=Finance` via `dsacls ... /G "CORP\Server-Admins:GA"`, framed realistically as leftover over-broad delegation from a past migration, never revoked. Confirmed via `Get-Acl "AD:\OU=Finance,..."` showing `CORP\Server-Admins / GenericAll / Allow`. This completes the escalation path designed in 5b: `mchen` (ordinary help-desk user, nothing notable on the account itself) → member of `Help-Desk` → nested in `IT-Admins` → nested in `Server-Admins` → `GenericAll` on `Finance` OU → can compromise `dbrooks`/`lkim`. Four hops, fully configured and validated end-to-end; actually exploiting it is left for a future attack-side exercise.

**5g, cached admin session — CLOSED 1 Oct 2026.** `CORP\Administrator` logged in interactively (console) on VM321, left active rather than logged off, per design (an active/recent privileged session is what keeps a credential realistically retrievable for a future compromised-workstation exercise; how such a credential would later be extracted is deliberately out of scope here — that's attack-tooling content for a future exercise, not build-step content). Confirmed via `quser`: `administrator`, session `console`, state `Active`, logon time 10/1/2026 4:49 PM.

**Item 5 (AD content population) is now fully CLOSED — all seven sub-steps (5a-5g) complete.** The combined DC01 hardening/configuration/content-population pass (items 1-5, originally the five dcdiag-era findings plus content population) is **entirely done.** Summary of what now exists in `corp.cyberrange.local`: a standard-shape OU tree (`Polaris` top-level, Admin Accounts/Service Accounts/Departments/Groups/Workstations/Servers, with IT/Finance/HR/Sales under Departments); six department users across those four departments; a four-hop nested-group privilege-escalation path (`mchen` → `Help-Desk` → `IT-Admins` → `Server-Admins`) terminating in real `GenericAll` rights on the Finance OU; a Kerberoastable service account (`cyber_svc`, SPN'd, RC4+AES, password-never-expires); an AS-REP roastable legacy account (`jthompson`); and a cached privileged credential on VM321 (`CORP\Administrator`, active console session). This is the realistic, exploitable content base the project's AD lab (DC01/VM321/VM322) was built to support, per §11.3.1's original design intent.

**11.3.4 Linux defense zone (VLAN 30) — execution log**

Roster confirmed 2 Oct 2026, per §11.3.1b's roadmap, with final hostnames assigned once VM332 was underway: VM330 metasploitable2, VM331 metasploitable3, **VM332 cyber-ubuntu01** (Ubuntu Server 26.04 LTS, general-purpose, standard clean→harden→snapshot pattern), **VM333 cyber-ubuntu02** (Ubuntu Server 26.04 LTS, privesc-focused — bad sudo/SUID/cron, introduced deliberately after hardening), **VM334 cyber-debian01** (Debian stable, Jenkins and GitLab CE together on one box), **VM335 cyber-alma01** (AlmaLinux, open-ended, reserved for future lateral-movement/scenario use, RHEL-family patch/package behavior).

**VM330, metasploitable2 — CLOSED 2 Oct 2026.** Built by importing the official pre-built `.vmdk` (SourceForge `Metasploitable2-Linux.zip`) directly via `qm importdisk` rather than the Proxmox GUI wizard, since no disk-size choice applies to an imported image (comes in at whatever size the source vmdk already is, historically ~8 GiB) and `qm importdisk` has no GUI equivalent. Built via CLI (`qm create` + `qm importdisk` + `qm set`) for consistency rather than mixing GUI-then-CLI-then-GUI. One real build quirk specific to this image's age: its 2.6.24/Ubuntu-8.04-era kernel predates in-guest virtio driver support, so **SeaBIOS (not UEFI/OVMF), IDE disk bus, and e1000 NIC model** were used instead of this project's usual virtio-everywhere pattern — a one-off exception for this host only, not a change to the project's standard build pattern.
- **Validated 2 Oct 2026:** DHCP addressing `10.10.30.179` (within the CYBER_LINUX pool), DNS search domain `linux.cyberrange.home.arpa` and resolver `10.10.30.1` both correct, outbound DNS/ping/HTTP all working, no global IPv6 present (link-local only, as expected). Protected-destination blocks confirmed via bash's `/dev/tcp` trick (adapted from the standard §12.1 validation block, since this image lacks `curl`/`ip`): OPNsense control plane (`10.10.30.1:443`), Proxmox (`192.168.10.2:8006`), CGNAT/Tailscale (`100.100.100.100:443`), and link-local (`169.254.169.254:80`) all returned `PASS: blocked`. Full zone-segmentation validation passed.
- **Snapshotted 2 Oct 2026.** No hardening pass applied or planned — ships vulnerable by design per operator agreement; this snapshot is the clean rollback point, not a hardened baseline.

**VM331, metasploitable3 — CLOSED 2 Oct 2026.** Obtained via Vagrant on kali01 (`vagrant box add rapid7/metasploitable3-ub1404 --provider virtualbox`, after resolving two blockers: Kali's default apt repos don't carry `vagrant` at all — HashiCorp's `.deb` was downloaded directly from `releases.hashicorp.com` and installed via `dpkg -i`, sidestepping the separate `kali-rolling`-codename incompatibility with HashiCorp's apt repo; and the initial `vagrant box add` command was missing the `/` in `rapid7/metasploitable3-ub1404`, which made Vagrant treat it as a literal local file rather than a Vagrant Cloud box reference). The resulting box's disk (`metasploitable3-ub1404-disk001.vmdk`, version `0.1.12-weekly`) was **not** transferred from kali01 directly — that transfer attempt confirmed cyber-fw01's isolation policy is working as designed (kali01, VLAN 10, cannot reach the `192.168.10.0/24` management network at all; `ping`/`scp` to the Proxmox host both failed outright). Instead, since the box is a public Vagrant Cloud artifact, the `.box` archive was downloaded directly onto the Proxmox host itself (`wget` + `tar -x`), avoiding any need for a firewall exception.
- Built via the standard `qm create`/`qm importdisk`/`qm set` CLI sequence, 2 cores / 2 GiB RAM. Unlike VM330, this image (Ubuntu 14.04-based) supports modern virtio drivers, so standard SeaBIOS/i440fx with **virtio-scsi-pci** disk bus and **virtio** NIC was used — no IDE/e1000 exception needed here.
- **Build correction logged:** the disk initially imported to the default `local` storage rather than `Cyber_VM` (the project's standard VM storage pool, confirmed via VM330's `Cyber_VM:vm-330-disk-0` placement). Caught before snapshotting; moved into place with `qm move_disk 331 scsi0 Cyber_VM`, which relocates an already-attached disk without needing to re-run `qm set`. Also corrected an invalid `--scsihw virtio-scsi-pcie` (not a real Proxmox enum value — correct value is `virtio-scsi-pci`).
- **Validated 2 Oct 2026:** DHCP addressing `10.10.30.152/24` (CYBER_LINUX pool), DNS search domain `linux.cyberrange.home.arpa` and resolver `10.10.30.1` both correct, outbound ping/`nslookup`/`curl` all working. Protected-destination blocks confirmed via standard `curl`-based probes (image has real tooling, unlike VM330): OPNsense control plane (`10.10.30.1`), Proxmox (`192.168.10.2:8006`), CGNAT/Tailscale (`100.100.100.100`), and link-local (`169.254.169.254`) all timed out as expected. Full zone-segmentation validation passed.
- **Snapshotted 2 Oct 2026** (`clean-build`). No hardening pass applied or planned — ships vulnerable by design, same as VM330.
- Default credentials: `vagrant`/`vagrant`.

**VM332, cyber-ubuntu01 (general-purpose) — CLOSED 2 Oct 2026.** Installed from `ubuntu-26.04-live-server-amd64.iso` (NAS ISO repo) via the Subiquity installer — entire-disk default LVM layout, OpenSSH server installed at install time, no featured server snaps. Built with the project's standard Linux-target pattern (SeaBIOS/i440fx, virtio-scsi-pci disk bus, virtio NIC) — no exceptions needed, unlike VM330. One build-process note: the initial `qm set --boot "order=ide2;scsi0"` failed because the semicolon was unquoted in the shell, splitting it into two commands and leaving `scsi0` as an invalid bare command; `order=ide2` alone still applied correctly and was sufficient to boot the installer, with the boot order corrected to `order=scsi0` and the install ISO detached after the OS was up.
- **Validated 2 Oct 2026:** DHCP addressing `10.10.30.105/24` (CYBER_LINUX pool), outbound ping/`nslookup`/`curl` all working, all four protected destinations (OPNsense, Proxmox, Tailscale/CGNAT, link-local) timed out as expected. `apt update && apt full-upgrade` run clean (65 packages).
- **Hardened 2 Oct 2026** — this is the first host in the Linux zone that isn't vulnerable-by-design, so it got a real baseline hardening pass rather than being snapshotted as-is: SSH root login disabled (`PermitRootLogin no`, confirmed via `grep`), UFW enabled with default-deny incoming / allow outgoing and only 22/tcp explicitly allowed, unattended-upgrades configured for automatic security updates (confirmed `enabled`), fail2ban installed and enabled for SSH brute-force protection. Service audit (`ss -tulpn`) post-hardening showed only expected services: SSH (22), local DNS stub resolver, DHCP client, NTP/chrony — no unexpected listeners.
- **Snapshotted 2 Oct 2026** (`hardened-baseline`) — this is the hardened baseline snapshot, not a pre-hardening clean snapshot, consistent with this being a non-vulnerable-by-design host.

**VM333, cyber-ubuntu02 (privesc-focused) — CLOSED 2 Oct 2026.** Built identically to VM332 (same ISO, same specs, standard virtio build pattern) and taken through the same full hardening pass (SSH root login disabled, UFW default-deny with only SSH allowed, unattended-upgrades enabled, fail2ban installed/enabled, fully patched, service-audited clean) and snapshotted as `hardened-baseline` before any vulnerability was introduced — preserving a clean non-vulnerable rollback point independent of the privesc chain layered on top.

Two-stage privilege-escalation chain designed and built deliberately after hardening, per operator request for a primary (easy) and secondary (hard, LinPEAS-relevant) finding on the same box:
- **Primary — sloppy sudo rule.** Low-privileged user `dcarter` created (`useradd -m`). Sudoers drop-in `/etc/sudoers.d/dcarter` grants `dcarter ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: /usr/bin/vim` (validated with `visudo -c`). A well-known GTFOBins entry — `sudo -l` immediately reveals it, and `sudo vim -c ':!/bin/sh'` drops straight to a root shell. Validated working 2 Oct 2026.
- **Secondary — custom SUID binary with a PATH-hijack vulnerability.** `/usr/local/bin/netcheck`, owned `root:root`, mode `4755`, framed as a leftover sysadmin diagnostic tool. Not a stock binary, so it doesn't appear in GTFOBins by name — discovery path is `find / -perm -4000 -type f` (or LinPEAS's automated SUID sweep flagging it as non-standard) followed by binary analysis (`strings`/`ltrace`) to notice it execs an unqualified `ping`, then a `$PATH` hijack to substitute a malicious `ping`.
  - **Build note — real debugging history worth keeping for reference:** the first implementation used `system("ping -c 1 8.8.8.8")`, which **did not work** — Ubuntu's `/bin/sh` (`dash`) resets its effective UID to the real UID on startup whenever they differ, a deliberate shell-level hardening that defeats `system()`-based SUID exploitation specifically. Confirmed via a zero-shell-involvement diagnostic payload (a compiled binary reporting `getuid()`/`geteuid()` directly) that still showed `euid=1001` even with no shell in the final hijacked step, proving the privilege was lost at `system()`'s internal `sh -c` invocation, not afterward. Fixed by rebuilding `netcheck` around `execlp("ping", ...)` instead — no shell anywhere in the vulnerable call, so there's nothing to drop the privilege. This is also the more realistic, more common real-world version of this vulnerability class (`system()`-based SUID shell-outs are a well-mitigated pattern; direct unqualified `execlp()`/`execvp()` is what LinPEAS's SUID analysis is really built to catch). Two further build slips along the way, both self-corrected: `sudo chown 4755` run in place of `sudo chmod 4755` (chown accepts a bare number as a literal UID — this momentarily set the binary's owner to a nonexistent UID 4755, which combined with a *previously*-set SUID bit on the original system()-based binary, produced an `euid=4755` result that incidentally confirmed the SUID exec mechanism itself was working correctly on this filesystem, ruling out a `nosuid` mount concern); and a `setresuid()` compile error (needs `_GNU_SOURCE` on glibc) in the final interactive shell-grab payload, resolved by using `setuid(0)` instead, which achieves the same real/effective/saved-UID result when called from an already-root-effective process.
  - Validated working 2 Oct 2026, including a full interactive root shell grab (`setuid(0); execl("/bin/bash","bash","-p",NULL)` as the hijacked `ping` payload) — confirmed via `whoami` returning `root`.
- **Post-validation cleanup:** all attacker-side testing scaffolding (`/tmp/.evil`, diagnostic payloads, shell history for both `dcarter` and `cyberadmin`) removed before the final snapshot, so the box starts in a genuine pre-discovery state for future exercises rather than with the solution already sitting on disk.
- **Snapshotted 2 Oct 2026** (`privesc-ready`) — the final vulnerable state, separate from the earlier `hardened-baseline` snapshot.

**VM334, cyber-debian01 (Jenkins + GitLab CE) — CLOSED 3 Oct 2026.** Installed Debian 13 ("trixie") from the NAS ISO repo — operator's only available bare-Debian media going into this build (previously only had Kali). Standard Linux-zone virtio build pattern, no exceptions needed. Operator's explicit design direction for this box, a deliberate departure from VM333's hand-built-vulnerability pattern: "realistically-patched but behind on previously unpatched CVEs" rather than scripted-in misconfiguration — simulating a host whose OS was kept current by an ops team while an app team's tooling (Jenkins, GitLab) drifted stale and fell off the patch radar. Operator confirmed the Jenkins CVE-2024-23897 + GitLab CE CVE-2021-22205 pairing (from the operator's own research) as "the right pair for this box."

- **Validated and hardened 3 Oct 2026,** identical pattern to VM332/333 (SSH root login disabled, UFW default-deny/SSH-only, unattended-upgrades, fail2ban — Debian, unlike Ubuntu, needed explicit `apt install` for all three since none ship pre-installed). Service audit surfaced CUPS (631) and avahi-daemon/mDNS (5353) — leftovers from the netinst "standard system utilities" task selection, not seen on VM332/333 — both disabled and purged, leaving SSH-only. `hardened-baseline` snapshot taken before any vulnerable software was installed.
- **Java prerequisite:** Debian 13's default repos ship only OpenJDK 21/25, not the 17 Jenkins 2.426.2 needs. Per operator's explicit preference ("try option 2 first"), installed Eclipse Temurin 17 from Adoptium's own apt repo rather than falling back to Java 21 — worked cleanly against the `trixie` codename on the first attempt, no `bookworm`-substitution fallback needed despite having it prepared.
- **Jenkins key-rotation discovery — real debugging history kept for reference:** Jenkins' documented `jenkins.io-2023.key` failed apt signature verification under Debian 13's new Sequoia (`sqv`)-based verifier with "Missing key" errors. Diagnosed in two stages: (1) `gpg --dearmor` was required regardless, since `sqv` — unlike the old `gpgv` — won't reliably parse an ASCII-armored keyring saved directly from Jenkins' site (same fix pattern as Adoptium); (2) even dearmored, the key still failed verification, and `gpg --show-keys` revealed the downloaded key had **expired 2026-03-26** — Jenkins rotated signing keys in December 2025 (jenkins.io blog: "Repository signing keys changing"), and the correct current key is `jenkins.io-2026.key` (fingerprint `5E386EADB55F01504CAE8EF7198F4B714ABFC68`). Once pulled and dearmored, apt updated clean and `apt-cache madison jenkins` listed `2.426.2` directly. Installed pinned (`apt install jenkins=2.426.2`) and held (`apt-mark hold jenkins`).
- **GitLab CE cross-codename install:** the target version (`13.8.7`, the last release before 13.8.8 patched CVE-2021-22205) predates Debian 13 by roughly four years, so GitLab's packagecloud repo-detection script was never going to serve it against `trixie` — packagecloud's own site and API also blocked this session's automated fetch tooling outright. Found and confirmed directly by the operator in a browser: `packages.gitlab.com/gitlab/gitlab-ce/debian/buster/pool/main/g/gitlab-ce/` lists `13.8.0`–`13.8.9` individually. Downloaded `gitlab-ce_13.8.7-ce.0_amd64.deb` directly (bypassing the repo-detection script entirely, same pattern as the earlier HashiCorp/Metasploitable3 `.deb` downloads) and installed via `dpkg -i` + `apt --fix-broken install` — the buster-era `.deb`'s dependencies resolved cleanly against trixie's package set with nothing missing. `gitlab-ctl reconfigure` completed clean (578/1546 resources, ~5 minutes). Held via `apt-mark hold gitlab-ce`.
  - Root password: GitLab 13.8.7 predates the auto-generated-password-to-file feature (added in 13.9) — no `/etc/gitlab/initial_root_password` is created on this version; root password is set interactively on first web visit instead. Confirmed by version history, not a missing-file bug.
  - **Puma/Jenkins port collision:** post-reconfigure, GitLab's web UI returned 502 and `gitlab-ctl status` showed Puma cycling through a new PID every few seconds — a crash loop, not a slow boot. `dmesg` showed no OOM-kill activity, ruling out the initial memory-pressure hypothesis (operator had already proactively disabled GitLab's bundled Prometheus/Grafana/Alertmanager/exporter stack via `prometheus_monitoring['enable'] = false`, a sound resource trim regardless but not the actual cause here). `gitlab-ctl tail puma` showed the real error: `Errno::EADDRINUSE ... port 8080` — GitLab's omnibus Puma defaults to binding TCP 8080 in addition to its unix socket, directly colliding with Jenkins, already listening there. Fixed by moving Puma off the conflicting port (`puma['port'] = 8081` in `/etc/gitlab/gitlab.rb`, reconfigure) — nginx continues serving the actual web UI on port 80 via the unix socket, unaffected.
- **Validated 3 Oct 2026:** Jenkins `2.426.2` active, held, listening on 8080, initial admin credentials retrieved. GitLab CE `13.8.7` active, held, root password set via first-visit web UI, dashboard loads clean.
- **Roster-wide finding surfaced during reachability testing, not a VM334-specific issue — see §7.4.1 for full detail.** Testing this box from kali-attack (VM310, VLAN 10) revealed that no cyber VLAN could reach any other cyber VLAN, a previously-unexercised consequence of existing, already-verified §7.4 policy. Resolved with a new scoped OPNsense pass rule (CYBER_ATTACK → CYBER_LINUX) plus opening `80/tcp`/`8080/tcp` in VM334's own UFW, which the OPNsense fix alone did not account for. Confirmed from kali-attack post-fix: GitLab (`302` → `/users/sign_in`) and Jenkins (`403`, `X-Jenkins: 2.426.2`) both reachable; OPNsense control plane (`10.10.30.1`) still correctly times out.
- **Snapshotted 3 Oct 2026** (`vulnerable-ready`) — final state, separate from the earlier `hardened-baseline` snapshot taken before Jenkins/GitLab installation.

**VM335, cyber-alma01 (lateral-movement/RHEL practice) — CLOSED 3 Oct 2026.** AlmaLinux 9.8 "Olive Jaguar", installed via Anaconda from the minimal ISO — first RHEL-family host and first use of Anaconda in this project (previously only Debian/Ubuntu's own installers). ISO sourced via the project's standard `NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange:iso/` read-only mount (§3.4.2), with one new valid sourcing route logged for the record: the file was fetched directly from AlmaLinux's own `9-latest` URL convention via the **homelab node's** Proxmox "Download from URL" feature (rather than a manual NAS-GUI upload, the route used for Kali/Parrot — see §11.2.0), landing on that node's `NAS-ISO-Repo` storage definition first. Confirmed via screenshot that the same file is visible read-only on the cyberrange node's `NAS-ISO-Repo-CyberRange` storage — same underlying NAS share, cyberrange side simply has no write access, so new ISOs must transit the homelab node (or the NAS's own GUI) before use here. Not a boundary violation; purely an access-path quirk worth this note.
- Built via the standard `qm create`/`qm set`/`qm start` CLI sequence, 2 cores/2 GiB RAM, standard Linux-zone virtio pattern (SeaBIOS/i440fx, virtio-scsi-pci, virtio NIC) — no exceptions needed.
- **Validated 3 Oct 2026:** DHCP addressing `10.10.30.141/24` (CYBER_LINUX pool), full standard zone-segmentation pattern (§12.1 block, OPNsense `.1` address substituted for VLAN 30) — all PASS.
- **Hardened 3 Oct 2026** — first RHEL-family hardening pass in this project, genuinely different tooling from the Debian/Ubuntu pattern used for VM332-334: SSH root login disabled; `firewalld` (not UFW) left active with only `ssh`/`dhcpv6-client` after removing an unexpected default `cockpit` allow-rule (package itself confirmed **not installed** via `rpm -q cockpit-ws cockpit-system` and `systemctl list-unit-files | grep -i cockpit`, both empty — same "trim unexpected default surface" discipline as VM334's CUPS/avahi cleanup); `SELinux` deliberately left in **Enforcing** mode (confirmed via `getenforce`/`sestatus`, targeted policy) rather than disabled, an explicit project decision for RHEL-family realism; `dnf-automatic` installed and enabled (`apply_updates = yes`) as the RHEL equivalent of unattended-upgrades; `fail2ban` installed via EPEL (`fail2ban-firewalld` backend, integrates with firewalld rather than raw iptables). Fully patched (`dnf update -y`, kernel `687.5.3` → `687.53.1`), rebooted before snapshotting so the baseline reflects the actually-running patched kernel, not just patched-on-disk. Post-reboot `ss -tulpn` clean (only `chronyd` on loopback and `sshd` on 22).
- **Snapshotted 3 Oct 2026** (`hardened-baseline`).

Vulnerability layer — operator's explicit direction: a mixture of all three offered VM335 angles rather than picking one.
- **Realism check on the originally-proposed CVE:** CVE-2017-7494 ("SambaCry") was the first candidate discussed, but ruled out on review — AlmaLinux 9 didn't exist until 2022, so a genuinely vulnerable 2017-era Samba binary on it has no plausible install story, and no unpatched decade-old Samba RCE is realistically found in the wild on a currently-maintained distro in 2026 regardless. Operator agreed; replaced with a realistic **misconfiguration** instead, which is also the far more common real-world Samba finding than an unpatched ancient RCE.
- **Samba misconfiguration built:** current patched `samba`/`samba-common`/`samba-client` installed via `dnf` (no version pinning or source compiling needed). New `[shared]` share added to `/etc/samba/smb.conf` at `/srv/shared` — `guest ok = yes`, `read only = no`, `create mask`/`directory mask = 0777`, `map to guest = Bad User` under `[global]` — fully anonymous, fully writable. SELinux context labeled `samba_share_t` via `semanage fcontext`/`restorecon` (left enforcing, not worked around) and the `samba_export_all_rw` boolean enabled, since `/srv/shared` isn't a user home directory. `firewalld` opened for the `samba` service.
- **Lateral-movement artifact:** an ed25519 keypair (`ops-deploy-key (legacy)`) generated directly on VM335 and planted at `/srv/shared/ops-tools/`, world-readable via the guest share — representing a realistic "ops team reused one deploy key across the fleet" finding. The matching public key was added to **VM332's** (`cyber-ubuntu01`, already closed/snapshotted) `cyberadmin` `authorized_keys` — explicitly reconfirmed with the operator before touching an already-closed host. See addendum below.
- **Validated end-to-end 3 Oct 2026 from kali-attack (VM310):** anonymous `smbclient` access to `//10.10.30.141/shared` confirmed, private key retrieved (`smbclient //10.10.30.141/shared -N -c 'cd ops-tools; get id_ed25519 /tmp/pivot_key'`), and the retrieved key used to SSH directly into VM332 as `cyberadmin@10.10.30.105` — full pivot proven working. No post-validation scaffolding to clean up, since the planted key is itself the intended find rather than attacker-side testing residue (unlike VM333's privesc scaffolding).
- **Snapshotted 3 Oct 2026** (`vulnerable-ready`) — final state, separate from the earlier `hardened-baseline` snapshot.

**Addendum to VM332's closure (above):** one `authorized_keys` line was added to `cyberadmin`'s account on 3 Oct 2026 in support of VM335's lateral-movement scenario (see above). No other change made, and no new snapshot taken on VM332 — its existing `hardened-baseline` snapshot remains the accurate pre-touch rollback point if this line is ever removed.

**Linux defense zone (VLAN 30) roster now fully CLOSED** — all six hosts (VM330-335) built and validated; VM332-335 also hardened and/or vulnerability-injected per their individual designs. Next zone per §11.3.1b: Apps zone (VM340, VLAN 40).

**11.3.5 Apps zone (VLAN 40) — execution log**

**VM340, cyber-apps01 (DVWA + Juice Shop) — CLOSED 4 Oct 2026.** Ubuntu Server 26.04 LTS installed from the NAS ISO repo, standard Linux-zone virtio build pattern (SeaBIOS/i440fx, virtio-scsi-pci, virtio NIC), 2 cores/4 GiB RAM/40 GiB disk — slightly more RAM than VM332's baseline to comfortably run two app containers. First zone built around **Docker rather than native installs** — operator's explicit direction: keeps the host OS itself in the same clean hardened-baseline pattern as every other Linux-zone host, while the actual vulnerable apps live in disposable containers that can be reset independently of the host. Domains/subdomains (part of the project's original "webhosted API with multiple levels of domains and subdomains" vision) explicitly deferred — apps reachable by plain IP:port for now, to be designed as a deliberate follow-on step rather than folded into this build.
- **Validated 4 Oct 2026:** standard zone-segmentation pattern (§12.1 block, OPNsense `.1` substituted for VLAN 40) — all PASS. DHCP addressing `10.10.40.121/24` (CYBER_APPS pool).
- **Hardened 4 Oct 2026** — same pattern as VM332 (SSH root login disabled, UFW default-deny/SSH-only initially, unattended-upgrades, fail2ban), since the host itself is not vulnerable-by-design; the vulnerability lives entirely in the containers layered on top. `hardened-baseline` snapshot taken before Docker was installed.
- **Docker install — real debugging history kept for reference:** Docker's standard apt-repo one-liner (`echo "deb [...] $(. /etc/os-release && echo $VERSION_CODENAME) stable" | tee ...`) initially produced `Malformed entry 1 in list file docker.list (Component)`. Diagnosed by checking `/etc/os-release` directly: `VERSION_CODENAME=resolute` and `UBUNTU_CODENAME=resolute` were both correctly set — Docker's own repo does carry a `resolute` dist (confirmed once fixed: `apt update` fetched `.../ubuntu resolute InRelease` cleanly, no fallback to an older codename needed). Root cause was quoting fragility, not a missing codename: nesting a double-quoted `echo "..."` inside the outer double-quoted `echo "deb [...] ... stable"` one-liner doesn't nest cleanly in bash, and a mangled first paste produced a blank codename field, shifting the line's fields and dropping the component. Re-running the identical corrected command resolved it — most likely a paste/line-wrap artifact on the first attempt, same general category as VM332's earlier unquoted-semicolon boot-order slip, not a real Ubuntu-26.04/Docker incompatibility.
- **`docker-compose.yml` — two further typos caught and fixed before first `up`:** (1) a YAML indentation slip — the Juice Shop service's `restart: unless-stopped` line was typed at 2-space indent (sibling of the service key) instead of 4-space (nested property), producing `go-yaml ... did not find expected key`; (2) the DVWA image name was typed `vulnerable/web-dvwa` (missing the "s") instead of the real Docker Hub image `vulnerables/web-dvwa`; also caught and corrected a `container-name` (hyphen) vs. the schema-required `container_name` (underscore) key on the Juice Shop service. File rewritten cleanly via heredoc rather than hand-patched, all three issues resolved in one pass.
- **Containers deployed 4 Oct 2026:** `docker compose up -d` succeeded — DVWA (`vulnerables/web-dvwa`, container `dvwa`, port `80:80`) and Juice Shop (`bkimminich/juice-shop`, container `juice-shop`, port `3000:3000`) both `Started`/`Up`. UFW opened for `80/tcp` and `3000/tcp`. `cyberadmin` deliberately **not** added to the `docker` group (that membership is root-equivalent); `sudo docker ...` used throughout instead, consistent with the project's least-privilege discipline elsewhere.
- **Attack-path exception added proactively this time** (new §7.4.3), as part of the build sequence rather than discovered missing afterward — applying the lesson from §7.4.1/§7.4.2 going forward.
- **Validated end-to-end 4 Oct 2026 from kali-attack (VM310):** OPNsense control plane (`10.10.40.1`) still blocked; DVWA returned `302` (expected first-boot redirect to its own `/setup.php`, not an error) and Juice Shop returned `200`. DVWA's one-time database initialization (`/setup.php` → "Create / Reset Database", default login `admin`/`password`) completed by the operator after the `vulnerable-ready` snapshot, so it isn't baked into the snapshot itself but is documented here so it isn't rediscovered as "broken" later — DVWA is immediately usable without that step on any future revert, it just shows the setup redirect until it's run once.
- **Snapshotted 4 Oct 2026** (`vulnerable-ready`) — final state, separate from the earlier `hardened-baseline` snapshot taken before Docker/the apps went on.

**Apps zone (VLAN 40) CLOSED** — first host built, validated, and reachable from the attack zone. Vulnhub-style boxes remain a future optional addition to this zone, not a blocking gap. Next per §11.3.1b: new Windows/Linux combination zone (VM370-379, new VLAN 70/CYBER_HYBRID) — requires real infrastructure work before any guest (OPNsense interface, DHCP scope, four-rule active-purpose policy, VM300 trunk-list update to admit tag 70).

### 11.4 Gate 8G4 — one-zone-at-a-time behavioral validation

1. Validate identity, single NIC, VLAN tag, DHCP address, .1 gateway, OPNsense DNS, and absence of global IPv6.
2. Confirm public DNS resolution and public HTTPS with curl -4 or the OS-specific equivalent.
3. Confirm OPNsense control-plane access is blocked except for approved DNS.
4. Confirm Proxmox 192.168.10.2:8006, CGNAT/Tailscale 100.100.100.100:443, and link-local 169.254.169.254:80 are blocked.
5. Correlate each block with OPNsense Live View using the guest's actual source IP.
6. Reconfirm Kali01 and Admin01 management continuity and leave the guest running only when the next setup step requires it.

### 11.5 Gate 8H — quarantine and scenario policy

Select one validated disposable workload for a powered-off temporary move to VLAN 99 while retaining its VMID.
Validate DHCP only, no manual DNS exception, no OPNsense control-plane access, no protected-network access, no Internet, and no global IPv6.
Return or rebuild the guest only through a separately approved cleanup path.
After standalone zones pass, design the minimum attack-to-target and target-to-SIEM ports. Place narrow scenario passes above PROTECTED_NETWORKS and scope them to exact aliases or hosts.
Never create a broad inter-zone allow and do not combine isolation validation with scenario-rule deployment. This includes any future narrow rule that would let attack-ai or defense-ai reach across zones — none is planned now; both AI guests stay local to their own zone.

**11.5.1 Captured design thought, 1 Oct 2026 — internal/scenario firewalls, not yet decided or built**

Operator raised a design question while mid-way through the DC01 hardening pass: cyber-fw01 currently does two very different jobs at once — permanent project containment (cyberrange/homelab/home isolation, explicitly the least-disposable, most-protected piece of this build) and, per the Gate 8H text above, the planned home for narrow scenario-specific pass rules. Operator's instinct: these shouldn't share one firewall, since routine scenario iteration on the same rule base that enforces containment is operationally risky, and a single perimeter-and-internal-segmentation firewall is also less realistic/valuable to practice against than genuine internal (east-west) segmentation. Operator explicitly wants **both** an internal firewall capability and the existing cyber-fw01, reasoning that mistakes on an internal firewall carry zero external risk (same disposable-by-design property every other guest already has, unlike cyber-fw01/kali01).

**Recommendation discussed (not yet approved or built):**
- **Split the concern in two.** Treat the VLAN topology and the internal firewall's placement as permanent infrastructure, built deliberately as its own bounded gate (same discipline as the original Gate 6 VLAN creation) — not improvised per scenario. Treat the internal firewall's *ruleset* as fully scenario-disposable (same free-to-break, free-to-restore property as every other non-cyber-fw01/kali01 guest), changed and reset as often as exercises need without touching cyber-fw01, Proxmox bridges, or VLAN tags.
- **Start with one placement, not all zones at once.** Recommended first candidate: split VLAN 20 into an "approach" sub-VLAN (VM321/VM322) and a "protected" sub-VLAN (DC01 moves behind the new firewall) — reuses the zone that's already built and populated, and lines up directly with the zone's existing Kerberoasting/BloodHound/lateral-movement design intent (§11.3.1): compromise a workstation, then have to defeat an internal firewall to reach the DC. Prove the pattern here first (new VLAN tag, cyber-fw01 trunk update to admit it, addressing-plan entry, new firewall VM sizing/placement), then replicate into Linux/Apps/SIEM zones as those get built, baking an internal-segmentation decision into each zone's own future build gate rather than retrofitting later.
- **Treat a standalone, not-permanently-wired "flex" internal firewall (operator's third option — movable, attachable to whichever zones as needed) as a separate, lower-priority backlog idea**, better suited to the operator's own "future cross-site" idea or one-off scenarios that don't fit the standard per-zone split, rather than the primary pattern.
- **Open, operator's call, not yet decided:** OPNsense again (consistency) vs. pfSense (product diversity, arguably more realistic given real orgs are often heterogeneous between edge and internal firewalls) for the internal firewall product.
- **Grounding fact for whenever this is designed concretely:** there is no way to insert an internal firewall between hosts that currently share one VLAN/subnet without splitting that VLAN into at least two — Proxmox's vmbr1 is a plain VLAN-aware L2 trunk today, with cyber-fw01 as the only L3 hop between any two zones, so a new VLAN tag is unavoidable regardless of which placement pattern is chosen.

**Status: discussion only, nothing built or decided.** Operator's explicit direction: revisit this properly when Gate 8H is actually reached, using this discussion as the starting point rather than re-deriving it from scratch.

### 11.5.2 Gate 8H implementation — scoped cross-zone passes for the SIEM zone

Distinct from the §11.5.1 internal-firewall-splitting idea (east-west segmentation via new VLANs, still a backlog discussion): this subsection records the concrete cross-zone **pass rules on the existing cyber-fw01** that the now-built SIEM zone needs, done in three operator-agreed sub-steps (8 Oct 2026), one at a time. Same pattern as the §7.4.1–7.4.4 attack-path exceptions — a scoped pass on the **source** zone's interface above the PROTECTED_NETWORKS block, plus a matching host-firewall (UFW) allow on the destination (the §11.3.4/VM334 defense-in-depth lesson), with positive **and** negative validation.

- **8H.1 — Dashboard access (VM360 → VM350) — CLOSED 8 Oct 2026.** Operator views the three SIEM UIs from the VM360 analyst workstation (VLAN 60), keeping with the "view on-box from the analyst zone, don't expose broadly" decision (§11.3.1i). Rules applied:
